, we put these two-level couplings together in a consistent inductive fashion to consider multilevel dynamics in interlacing arrays in Propositions
6.1
and
6.15
. This proves Theorem
2.24
, the probabilistic statement of Theorem
2.14
and allows for the computation of the explicit correlation functions in Theorems
2.9
and
2.14
in the next section. The induction (given the two-level couplings), making use of the Markov functions theory of Rogers-Pitman
[
126
]
, by virtue of the intertwinings obtained previously, is standard and variants thereof can be found in multiple places in the literature
[
134
,
24
,
114
,
35
,
12
,
6
,
121
,
5
]
. The space-level inhomogeneous setting of Theorem
2.24
is a little more involved to handle in a systematic way but still the main work was done in the preceding sections.

The computation of the correlation functions from Theorem
2.9
and
2.14
is done in Section
7
. The fact that the point processes in question have determinantal correlation functions is a consequence of the results of Section
6
and the celebrated Eynard-Mehta theorem
[
66
,
37
,
26
]
. The explicit computation of the correlation kernel then boils down to solving a certain biorthogonalisation problem. To do this we use in an essential way the contour integral formulae, for all the quantities involved, in terms of the polynomials
p_{x}(z)
. Making use of the results from Section
3
all the computations become rather neat. An analogous but simpler computation, in fact a special case, was performed in
[
8
]
. That paper deal with continuous-time dynamics only and its main result is the case
f(w)=e^{-tw}
of Theorem
2.9
. Finally, the literature on different biorthogonalisation problems arising from the Eynard-Mehta theorem is vast, we list a very small sample
[
28
,
24
,
23
,
26
,
8
,
6
]
which seems most relevant.

In Section
8
, we prove the probabilistic representation of
\left(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}\right)_{t\geq 0}
as independent walks conditioned to never intersect using a soft argument. In the special case of the space being homogeneous this boils down to essentially equivalent arguments (although presented a bit differently), which first appeared in
[
112
,
17
]
. The proof relies in an essential way on the fact that walks with different drifts (realised through a Doob transform) become asymptotically ordered with probability one depending on the relative strength of their drifts, see (
103
). When the walks are identical this is of course no longer true and the argument breaks down. One would hope though that the model of identical walks can be recovered as the limit of removing the drifts, under possibly additional conditions on
\mathbf{a}
. There are some subtle points that need to be taken care of to make this rigorous and we leave it for future work. When the increments of the walks are not location-dependent there is vast literature on non-intersection probabilities, see
[
58
,
63
,
95
]
for a small sample, but the idea used therein of coupling with Brownian motion does not appear useful here.

In Section
9
, we prove a precise version of Theorem
2.32
. First, to get the explicit form of the transition kernels
\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{r}}^{(N)}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{l}}^{(N)}
presented in Theorem
9.5
, we develop a space-inhomogeneous extension of the original idea of Dieker and Warren
[
60
]
, which itself deals with the level-inhomogeneous setting, see also
[
18
]
. This approach makes use of intertwinings and inverting certain Markov kernels (when viewed as stochastic matrices) that are given as determinants. A non-intersecting path model and the LGV formalism
[
69
]
to obtain equivalent combinatorial expressions for these kernels is essential for our argument. The resulting explicit formula is of what is usually referred to in the literature as Schutz-type, in reference to Schutz’s original work on the transition probabilities of TASEP
[
128
]
. Schutz’s original derivation
[
128
]
of the formula for TASEP used the Bethe ansatz instead. Once one has an explicit formula of this type, then to obtain determinantal correlation functions one goes through a procedure usually referred to as Sasamoto’s trick
[
127
,
26
,
23
,
27
]
of rewriting this formula as a sum over interlacing arrays. By virtue of the intertwining origins of our formula from Theorem
9.5
such determinantal correlations are essentially an immediate consequence as shown in Theorem
9.6
. This is both conceptually more satisfying and computationally cleaner compared to the rewriting involved in the Sasamoto’s trick approach
[
127
,
26
,
23
,
27
]
but on the other hand requires a lot of machinery to have been developed already.

In Section
10
, we establish Theorem
2.37
. We first prove consistency for
\left(\mathcal{M}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}(\cdot;\mathbf{a})\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
and that they are indeed probability measures by observing the connection (
23
) with the dynamics we have been studying. Then, to prove extremality we make use of a well-adapted generating function for the measures
\left(\mathcal{M}^{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{N}(\cdot;\mathbf{a})\right)_{N=1}^{\infty}
and De-Finetti’s theorem
[
3
]
. As far as we can tell, in this context, this type of argument originates with
[
116
]
and variants of it have been employed in the literature a few times. However, a number of rather special ingredients need to be present in order for it to work, as can be seen from the rather long proof, and the fact that it does in our case as well is not obvious a-priori. This section is also the only place where the factorial Schur functions
[
100
]
make their appearance explicitly. In fact, a number of, although far from all, the results in this paper can be phrased and proven in terms of factorial Schur functions which would be more in the spirit of developing the factorial Schur analogue of the Schur process
[
117
]
and Schur dynamics
[
20
]
framework. Instead we wanted to emphasize the inhomogeneous Toeplitz-matrix perspective which is somewhat more probabilistic in nature. For example, as far as we can tell, the couplings coming from coalescing walks in Section
5
(for example
\mathsf{Q}_{f}^{N,N+1}
) do not have a natural interpretation in terms of symmetric functions.

In Section
11
, we prove Theorem
2.44
. As far as we can tell, no results of this kind have appeared in the literature before; even the fully homogeneous case appears to be new. Our initial motivation stemmed from the following. In an interesting paper
[
122
]
, Petrov showed how one could obtain determinantal formulae for the inhomogeneous-space push-TASEP in continuous-time with fully-packed initial condition via a connection to the original level-inhomogeneous (but space-homogeneous) model of Borodin-Ferrari
[
24
]
. We then wanted to understand whether a duality of sorts between space and level inhomogeneities extended to dynamics on full arrays (and not just the right edge), for more general initial conditions and also for other types of dynamics in discrete time. This led to Theorem
2.44
. The proof itself is elementary and, as long as one takes the right perspective, not difficult.

Our results in Section
12
generalise the work of Nordenstam
[
111
]
, where for the first time the dynamics of the shuffling algorithm on Aztec diamonds, see
[
64
,
65
,
123
]
, in the case of uniform weights, were found to be connected to interlacing particle systems with push-block dynamics. In order to do this we reinterpret and combine the aforementioned work of Nordenstam
[
111
]
and Propp
[
123
]
where the shuffling algorithm was introduced for general weights
\mathcal{W}
on Aztec diamonds. Then, to prove Theorem
2.48
we first show that the weights from Definition
2.47
are consistent
15
15

15

The reader will have surely noticed by now that consistency of different types is an overarching theme of this paper.
in the sense of Definition
12.10
and then make use of our previous results. An extension of the work of Nordenstam in the language of line ensembles (equivalent to tilings of the Aztec diamond, see
[
78
,
79
,
25
,
62
]
, also Sections
2.8
and
13
) to some other weights beyond the uniform one can be found in
[
25
]
. However, general weights
\mathcal{W}
on Aztec diamonds, see Section
12
, or even the more special weight from Theorem
2.48
, as far as we can tell, are not considered in
[
25
]
. In particular, our results cannot be obtained from
[
25
]
. It would be interesting to consider dynamics on line ensembles as in
[
25
]
induced by a general weight
\mathcal{W}
but we do not do it here. Finally, as already mentioned, tilings of Aztec diamonds and also dynamics coming from shuffling-type algorithms have been much studied in the literature
[
79
,
51
,
48
,
39
,
38
,
62
,
16
,
22
,
45
,
14
,
15
,
46
,
47
,
50
,
49
]
but the techniques and results of all these papers are different from what we do here.

Our work in Section
13
on line ensembles with fixed starting and end points follows closely the methods of
[
62
,
16
]
. We show how some of their results can be generalised to the inhomogeneous Toeplitz-like matrix setting with a matrix symbol in Theorems
13.4
and
13.7
, from which Theorem
2.50
easily follows. The exact fixed
N
results from
[
62
]
allow for a rather clean adaptation, while the asymptotics relevant for the limit of the bottom paths are more involved due to the inhomogeneity
\mathbf{a}
. The main tools in this study are matrix-valued orthogonal polynomials and the analysis of the associated Riemann-Hilbert problems. This part of the paper is only a small step in developing the relevant theory (a proof of concept that something can be done) and many questions remain wide open: for example the probabilistic significance of
\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}}
for general matrix-valued
\mathbf{f}
, whether various multidimensional constructions for scalar
f
studied earlier in the paper have analogues for matrix
\mathbf{f}
, and also asymptotics of the line ensembles in different scaling regimes which are considered in
[
62
]
(corresponding to the gas/smooth phase of the two-periodic Aztec diamond for example). There are also very interesting subsequent works
[
14
,
45
,
22
,
15
]
which use some of the machinery of
[
62
,
16
]
. There might be suitable adaptations of those results to our setup but this is purely speculative at present.

In Section
14
, the short-time convergence of the continuous-time dynamics, having general inhomogeneity
\mathbf{a}
, to the discrete Bessel determinantal point process is proven using some standard asymptotic analysis of the correlation kernel. The only noteworthy point is that, in this particular scaling, the rescaled polynomials
p_{x}(z)
converge to an exponential function depending on
\mathbf{a}
only through the average

\bar{a}
, see (
147
), which is really what makes the proof work.

Organisation of the paper

In Section
3
we develop some theory for inhomogeneous Toeplitz-like matrices and the corresponding one-dimensional Markov dynamics. In Section
4
we discuss dynamics for non-intersecting paths (single levels of the arrays) and prove a key intertwining relation. In Section
5
we consider various couplings for two sets of non-intersecting paths (two levels of the array). In Section
6
we put everything together to consider consistent dynamics on arrays and also prove Theorem
6.2
on parameter symmetry. In Section
7
we compute the correlation kernels from Section
2.2
. In Section
8
we prove Theorem
2.28
on conditioned walks. In Section
9
we prove a precise version of Theorem
2.32
, for the edge particle systems, see Theorems
9.5
and
9.6
. In Section
10
we prove Theorem
2.37
on extremal coherent measures. In Section
11
we prove the duality results from Section
2.6
. In Section
12
we explain the connection between the shuffling algorithm for sampling arbitrary weightings of the Aztec diamond and certain Bernoulli push-block dynamics and prove Theorem
2.48
. In Section
13
, we develop a framework generalising some of the results of
[
62
]
and
[
16
]
in order to prove Theorem
2.50
. Finally, in Section
14
we prove Theorem
2.20
on convergence to the discrete Bessel point process.

3

One-dimensional dynamics and inhomogeneous Toeplitz-like matrices

3.1

Inhomogeneous Toeplitz-like matrices

Given
R>0
we use the following notation for the half plane
\mathbb{H}_{-R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\Re(z)>-R\}
. We write
\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
for the set of functions which are holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{-R}
. Recall that a function
f
is entire if it is holomorphic in the whole of
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
we define the following quantities, where recall that the counterclockwise contour
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}\subset\mathbb{H}_{-R}
encircles all the points of the sequence
\mathbf{a}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{y}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{p_{y+1}(w)}f(w)dw,\ \ x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

(30)

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{f}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{f}(0,x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{x}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{f(w)}{p_{x+1}(w)}dw,\ \ x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(31)

Clearly,
\mathsf{T}_{f}
depends on
\mathbf{a}
but we supress it from the notation since
\mathbf{a}
is fixed. Observe that, in the homogeneous case
a_{x}\equiv 1
,
[\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)]_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
is nothing but the Toeplitz matrix with symbol
f(1-z)
. For this reason we shall call
\mathsf{T}_{f}
the inhomogeneous Toeplitz-like matrix/operator with symbol
16
16

16

We prefer this terminology to the somewhat more precise “… with symbol
f(1-z)
”.

f
.

We note that the only poles for the integrand in the definition of
\mathsf{T}_{f}
come from the inhomogeneity
\mathbf{a}
. In particular, we note that
\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)\equiv 0
, for
x>y
, namely the matrix
[\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)]_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
is upper-triangular. From a probabilistic standpoint, for certain special choices of
f
,
\mathsf{T}_{f}
will be the transition probability, from
x
to
y
, of a Markov chain on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
which moves only to the right.

More general functions
f
, which are not in
\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
, can be used to define
\mathsf{T}_{f}
and most of the results below have corresponding analogues. We restrict to
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
for simplicity since they suffice for the probabilistic applications we have in mind.

For functions
g:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
(equivalently sequences in
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
), for which the sum converges, we write
\mathsf{T}_{f}g(x)=\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)g(y)
. It is easy to see that the operator
\mathsf{T}_{f}
is well-defined on bounded sequences but it is actually defined on a somewhat larger space, see Proposition
3.8
.

In fact, as we show in Proposition
3.10
, the matrix
[\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)]_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
, with general sequence
\mathbf{a}
, is similar to the standard Toeplitz matrix (namely with
a_{x}\equiv 1
) with symbol
f(1-z)
using an explicit change of basis matrix. However, even in the one-dimensional setting of this section it is not possible, using this similarity, to simply translate results from the standard Toeplitz matrix setting to the inhomogeneous one, see Remark
3.11
. In the multidimensional setting of Sections
4
,
5
and
6
it is unclear whether this matrix similarity is of any use at all but it would be interesting if something can be done using it.

We finally define the adjoint kernel by
\mathsf{T}_{f}^{*}(x,y)=\mathsf{T}_{f}(y,x)
and corresponding operator
\mathsf{T}_{f}^{*}g(x)=\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y,x)g(y)
. Clearly, by its lower-triangular structure,
\mathsf{T}_{f}^{*}
is well-defined on the whole of
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
. From a probabilistic standpoint
\mathsf{T}_{f}^{*}
evolves measures on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
according to the dynamics of the Markov chain governed by
\mathsf{T}_{f}
.

The following constant will appear often throughout the paper.

Definition 3.2
.

Given an inhomogeneity sequence
\mathbf{a}
, define
R(\mathbf{a})
by

R(\mathbf{a})=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}-\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}.

(32)

We have the following expansion of functions
f
in terms of the polynomials
p_{x}(z)
. When
a_{x}\equiv 1
,
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, this is simply the Taylor expansion of the function
f
around 1.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
and
\mathcal{U}
be a compact set in
\mathbb{H}_{-R}
for some
R>0
. Suppose there exists a (counterclockwise) contour
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}\subset\mathbb{H}_{-R}
containing
\{a_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
such that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\frac{\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}|u-a_{k}|}{\inf_{w\in\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}|w-a_{k}|}=r<1.

(33)

Then, the following expansion converges absolutely and uniformly for
u\in\mathcal{U}
,

f(u)=\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}p_{x}(u)\mathsf{T}_{f}(x).

(34)

If
f
is entire then for any compact
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{C}
a contour
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
satisfying (
33
) exists and so (
34
) converges uniformly on compact sets in
\mathbb{C}
. Finally, suppose
R>R(\mathbf{a})
. Then, for
\epsilon>0
small enough and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\epsilon}
given by the rectangle

\mathcal{U}=\left\{u\in\mathbb{C}:-\epsilon\leq\Re{u}\leq\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k},-\epsilon\leq\Im(u)\leq\epsilon\right\}

(35)

a contour
\mathsf{C}_{a}
satisfying (
33
) exists.

Proof.

Observe that,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{a_{y}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathsf{a}}}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{p_{y+1}(w)}dw=\mathbf{1}_{x=y}.

Thus, we get that with
f(u)=p_{y}(u)
, and more generally by linearity for
f(u)
any polynomial, the expansion (
34
), which is a finite sum, converges uniformly and absolutely for all
u\in\mathbb{C}
.

We now assume that
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
is picked as in the statement. Recall that
f
is analytic in
\mathbb{H}_{-R}
. Take
f_{N}(u)
to be the truncated degree
N
Taylor polynomial in the Taylor expansion of
f(u)
about a point
u_{*}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. This expansion converges in the open disk of radius
R+u_{*}
centred at
u_{*}
. By taking
u_{*}
large enough we can get this disk to contain both
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
and thus for the expansion to converge on
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
. Moreover note that, from the observation in the paragraph above we have,

\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}p_{x}(u)\mathsf{T}_{f_{N}}(x)=\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}-\frac{1}{a_{x}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}p_{x}(u)\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{f_{N}(w)}{p_{x+1}(w)}dw=f_{N}(u).

(36)

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
be fixed. We then have the following bound, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
(x,w)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
, by using (
33
) and the fact that
f_{N}
converges uniformly to
f
in
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
and thus it is uniformly bounded,

\left|\frac{1}{a_{x}}\frac{p_{x}(u)f_{N}(w)}{p_{x+1}(w)}\right|\lesssim\prod_{k=0}^{x}\frac{|u-a_{k}|}{|w-a_{k}|}\lesssim r^{x}.

Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem we have, for fixed
u\in\mathcal{U}
,

\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}p_{x}(u)\mathsf{T}_{f_{N}}(u)\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}p_{x}(u)\mathsf{T}_{f}(u).

On the other hand, we know that
f_{N}(u)\to f(u)
uniformly for
u\in\mathcal{U}
and this proves the expansion for fixed
u\in\mathcal{U}
. We now show that the series converges uniformly and absolutely in
\mathcal{U}
. We can bound for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
, using (
33
) and the fact that
f
is uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{U}
,

\displaystyle\left|p_{x}(u)\mathsf{T}_{f}(x)\right|\lesssim\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\prod_{k=0}^{x}\frac{|u-a_{k}|}{|w-a_{k}|}dw\lesssim r^{x}.

By the Weirstrass M-test the desired conclusion follows.

When
f
is entire then for any compact
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{C}
we can take
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
a circle of very large radius centred at the origin and (
33
) is seen to hold. Finally, suppose
R>R(\mathbf{a})
and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\epsilon}
is as in (
35
). We claim that taking
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}\subset\mathbb{H}_{-R}
a rectangular contour with sides parallel to the real and imaginary axes with the left side being part of the line
\Re(w)=-R+\epsilon
, the right side part of the line
\Re(w)=M
and the upper and lower sides parts of the lines
\Im(w)=\pm M
respectively for some large
M
works. See Figure
8
for an illustration of this contour. For such
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
we have, by taking
\epsilon
small enough,

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\frac{\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}}|u-a_{k}|}{\inf_{w\in\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}|w-a_{k}|}\leq\frac{\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)}{\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+R+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)}<\frac{\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}}{\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+R(\mathbf{a})}=1.

This concludes the proof.

\Re\left(w\right)=-R
\Re\left(w\right)=-R+\epsilon
\Im\left(w\right)=M
\Im\left(w\right)=-M
\Re(w)=M
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
0
-\epsilon
\sup_{k}a_{k}

Figure 8:
The contour (not to scale) used in the proof of Lemma
3.3
associated to the rectangle
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{\epsilon}
from (
35
).

∎

We have the following composition property for the
\mathsf{T}_{f}
kernels. In the probabilistic setting this is simply the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
f,g\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
with
R>R(\mathbf{a})
. Then, we have

\mathsf{T}_{f}\mathsf{T}_{g}=\mathsf{T}_{fg}.

(37)

Proof.

We compute, by deforming the
u
contour from
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
to a contour
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}\subset\mathcal{U}
where
\mathcal{U}
is the region defined in (
35
), which can be done without crossing any poles, and making use of Lemma
3.3
and then deforming back to
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{f}\mathsf{T}_{g}(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{a_{y}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{g(u)}{p_{y+1}(u)}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}p_{m}(u)\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{p_{x}(w)f(w)}{p_{m+1}(w)}dwdu

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{a_{y}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{p_{x}(u)}{p_{y+1}(u)}f(u)g(u)du=\mathsf{T}_{fg}(x,y),

as desired.
∎

We have the following normalisation result for
\mathsf{T}_{f}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
. For any
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)=f(0).

Proof.

Observe that since
f
has no poles in
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
,
\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)=0
for
y\leq x
. Then, we deform
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
to a contour
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}\subset{\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}}
such that

\sup_{w\in\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\left|\frac{a_{k}}{a_{k}-w}\right|=r<1.

(38)

Such a contour is always possible to find. We can take a rectangular contour with sides parallel to the real and imaginary axes with the left side being part of the line
\Re(w)=-\epsilon^{\prime}
for some
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
, the right side part of the line
\Re(w)=M
and the upper and lower sides parts of the lines
\Im(w)=\pm M
respectively for some very large
M
. See Figure
9
for an illustration. Then, we have uniformly for
w\in\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}
,

\sum_{y>x}\frac{1}{a_{m}p_{y+1}(w)}=-\frac{1}{wp_{x+1}(w)}.

(39)

This follows, after some relabelling, by taking the
x\to\infty
limit in the elementary identity, by virtue of the bound (
38
) above for
w\in\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}
,

\sum_{k=0}^{x}\frac{1}{a_{k}}\prod_{i=0}^{k}\frac{a_{i}}{a_{i}-w}=-\frac{1}{w}\left(1-\prod_{i=0}^{x}\frac{a_{i}}{a_{i}-w}\right).

(40)

Thus we obtain, where we deform to the
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}
contour without crossing any poles,

\displaystyle\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)=\sum_{y=x}^{\infty}\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\left[\frac{1}{wp_{x+1}(w)}-\frac{1}{a_{x}p_{x+1}(w)}\right]p_{x}(w)f(w)dw

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{f(w)}{w}dw.

Evaluating the integral by picking the residue at
0
gives the result.

\Re\left(w\right)=-\epsilon
\Re\left(w\right)=-\epsilon^{\prime}
\Im\left(w\right)=M
\Im\left(w\right)=-M
\Re(w)=M
\inf_{k}a_{k}
0
\sup_{k}a_{k}

Figure 9:
The contour (not to scale) used in the proof of Lemma
3.5
.

∎

For
g:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
define the forward and backward discrete derivatives
\nabla^{+}
and
\nabla^{-}
by

\nabla^{+}g(x)=g(x+1)-g(x),\ \ \nabla^{-}g(x)=g(x-1)-g(x).

We have the following relation for
\mathsf{T}_{f}
that we call the duality relation (the terminology is because this relation is somewhat reminiscent to dualities for Markov processes
[
76
]
and in particular the Siegmund duality
[
129
]
; however it is actually not a case of a Siegmund duality or any Markov duality for that matter).

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
. We then have

-\frac{a_{x}}{a_{y}}\nabla_{x}^{+}\mathsf{T}_{f}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y\rrbracket}(x)=\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y).

(41)

Proof.

Observe that, from Lemma
3.5
we have

\nabla_{x}^{+}\mathsf{T}_{f}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y\rrbracket}(x)=-\nabla_{x}^{+}\sum_{m>y}\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,m).

On the other hand, using identity (
40
), after deforming first to the contour
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}
as in the proof of Lemma
3.5
and then to
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}
, we get that the last display is equal to

-\nabla_{x}^{+}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{p_{x}(w)f(w)}{wp_{y+1}(w)}dw.

Bringing the discrete derivative
\nabla^{+}_{x}
inside the integral, using that

a_{x}\nabla_{x}^{+}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{w}=-p_{x}(w)

and then deforming back to
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
gives the result.
∎

Finally, we note that, for fixed
\lambda
, the function
x\mapsto p_{x}(\lambda)
is an eigenfunction of
\mathsf{T}_{f}
with explicit eigenvalue.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
with
R>R(\mathbf{a})
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}
the set defined in (
35
). Then, the function
h_{\lambda}(x)=p_{x}(\lambda)
is an eigenfunction of
\mathsf{T}_{f}
with eigenvalue
f(\lambda)
,

\mathsf{T}_{f}h_{\lambda}(x)=f(\lambda)h_{\lambda}(x),\ \ \forall x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(42)

If
f
is entire then the above holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

This is an application of Lemma
3.3
with the function
f(z)h_{z}(x)=f(z)p_{x}(z)
.
∎

In the rest of this subsection we collect some general results and comments about the operator/matrix
\mathsf{T}_{f}
. These will not be used in any of our probabilistic applications but they are interesting in their own right so we present them here. Define the following spaces
\ell_{\textnormal{exp},M}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
, where
M\in(1,\infty)
, and
\ell_{\textnormal{exp}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
by,

\displaystyle\ell_{\textnormal{exp},M}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(g(k)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}:\exists\ C_{M}<\infty\textnormal{ such that for all }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},|g(y)|\leq C_{M}M^{y}\right\},

\displaystyle\ell_{\textnormal{exp}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(g(k)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}:\exists\ M\in(0,\infty)\textnormal{ such that }\left(g(k)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\in\ell_{\textnormal{exp},M}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)\right\}.

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon
. Then, there exists
r<1
and
C_{f,r}<\infty
such that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have

\left|\mathsf{T}_{f}\left(x,y\right)\right|\leq C_{f,r}r^{|y-x|}.

In particular,
\mathsf{T}_{f}
is well-defined acting on
\ell_{\textnormal{exp},M}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
for
M<r^{-1}
. If
f
is entire then for any
0<\delta<1
there exists
C_{f,\delta}<\infty
such that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\left|\mathsf{T}_{f}\left(x,y\right)\right|\leq C_{f,\delta}\delta^{|y-x|},

and so in this case
\mathsf{T}_{f}
is well-defined acting on
\ell_{\textnormal{exp}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
.

Proof.

For the first statement we can deform the
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
contour to the rectangle from the proof of Lemma
3.5
, see also Figure
9
. By taking the absolute value inside the integral we obtain the desired conclusion. When
f
is entire we can deform
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
to a very large circle from which, after bringing the absolute value inside the integral, the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

We give a more abstract interpretation of
\mathsf{T}_{f}^{*}
viewed as an operator from a certain sequence space to itself when
f
is entire. Define the space
\ell_{\textnormal{hol}}
by,

\ell_{\textnormal{hol}}=\left\{\left(g(k)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}:\forall\ 0<r<1,\ \exists\ C_{r}<\infty\textnormal{ such that for all }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},|g(y)|\leq C_{r}r^{y}\right\}.

Observe that, we have the inclusions, with
M,q\geq 1
, where
\ell^{q}
are the usual sequence spaces,

\ell_{\textnormal{hol}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)\subset\ell^{q}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)\subset\ell_{\textnormal{exp},M}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)\subset\ell_{\textnormal{exp}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right).

Now, we consider the map
\mathcal{V}
given by, where
\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{C}\right)
denotes the space of entire functions,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}:\ell_{\textnormal{hol}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{C}\right),

\displaystyle\left(g(y)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}

\displaystyle\mapsto h(z)=\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}g(y)p_{y}(z),

which by the preceding results of this section it is well-defined and moreover it is in fact a bijection with inverse given by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{-1}:\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{C}\right)

\displaystyle\to\ell_{\textnormal{hol}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right),

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(-\frac{1}{a_{y}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{h(z)}{p_{y+1}(z)}dz\right)_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}=\left(\mathsf{T}_{h}(y)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}.

The fact that this sequence has the correct decay properties again follows by deforming
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
to a very large circle. Given
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{C}\right)
define the multiplication operator
\mathsf{Mult}_{f}:\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{C}\right)\to\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{C}\right)
by
\mathsf{Mult}_{f}\left(h\right)=fh
. Then, we can see that we have the following representation of
\mathsf{T}_{f}^{*}:\ell_{\textnormal{hol}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)\to\ell_{\textnormal{hol}}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
:

\mathsf{T}_{f}^{*}=\mathcal{V}^{-1}\mathsf{Mult}_{f}\mathcal{V}.

(43)

We now prove that the matrix
[\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)]_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
, with general sequence
\mathbf{a}
, is similar to the standard Toeplitz matrix (namely with
a_{x}\equiv 1
) with symbol
f(1-z)
using an explicit change of basis matrix. We restrict to entire
f
for simplicity. The result below can also be derived from (
43
) but we give a direct computational proof which is instructive.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
f
be entire. Let
\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)
be as in (
30
) with general and fixed
\mathbf{a}
and denote by
\tilde{\mathsf{T}}_{f}(x,y)
the homogeneous case (standard Toeplitz case) of (
30
):

\tilde{\mathsf{T}}_{f}(x,y)=\tilde{\mathsf{T}}_{f}(y-x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{|1-z|=1}\frac{f(z)(1-z)^{x}}{(1-z)^{y+1}}dz=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{|z|=1}f(1-z)z^{x-y-1}dz.

Moreover, consider the matrix
\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{a})
with entries given by, with
k,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\mathbf{A}_{km}(\mathbf{a})=(-1)^{k-m}\left(\prod_{l=0}^{k-1}a_{l}^{-1}\right)e_{k-m}\left(1-a_{0},1-a_{1},\dots,1-a_{k-1}\right)

(44)

where
e_{l}
is the
l
-th elementary symmetric polynomial:

e_{l}(z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{N})=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{l}\leq N}z_{j_{1}}z_{j_{2}}\cdots z_{j_{l}}.

Then, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we have

\mathsf{T}_{f}\left(x,y\right)=\left[\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{a})\tilde{\mathsf{T}}_{f}\mathbf{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})\right](x,y).

(45)

Proof.

Observe that, both
\left\{(1-z)^{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
and
\left\{p_{x}(z)\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
are bases in the ring of polynomials
\mathbb{C}[z]
. By virtue of Vieta’s formulae and (
44
) we can see that
\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{a})
is actually the change of basis matrix, and in particular also invertible,

\displaystyle p_{k}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\mathbf{A}_{km}(\mathbf{a})(1-z)^{m},

(46)

\displaystyle(1-z)^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{km}(\mathbf{a})p_{m}(z).

(47)

Then, using (
46
), we can write

\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{y}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{f(z)p_{x}(z)}{p_{y+1}(z)}dz=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\mathbf{A}_{xm}(\mathbf{a})\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{y}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{f(z)(1-z)^{m}}{p_{y+1}(z)}dz\right).

By virtue of Lemma
3.3
, since
f
is entire, expanding
f(z)(1-z)^{m}
as a series in two ways

\displaystyle f(z)(1-z)^{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{|1-z|=1}\frac{f(z)(1-z)^{m}}{(1-z)^{k+1}}dz\right)(1-z)^{k}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\tilde{\mathsf{T}}_{f}(m,k)(1-z)^{k},

\displaystyle f(z)(1-z)^{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{k}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{f(z)(1-z)^{m}}{p_{k+1}(z)}dz\right)p_{k}(z),

using (
47
) and comparing coefficients for
p_{y}(z)
we get

-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{y}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{f(z)(1-z)^{m}}{p_{y+1}(z)}dz=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\tilde{\mathsf{T}}_{f}(m,l)\mathbf{A}_{ly}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})

and this completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Using Proposition
3.10
and assuming the corresponding result for the standard Toeplitz setting, namely
a_{x}\equiv 1
, we can give quick alternative proofs of Lemmas
3.4
and
3.7
for general
\mathbf{a}
(and entire
f
). It is also possible to prove Lemma
3.5
for general
\mathbf{a}
in the same way if we observe that
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}_{xm}^{-1}(\mathbf{a})=1
, for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. However, the important (later on) duality formula from Lemma
3.6
does not seem to follow along these lines.

3.2

One-dimensional dynamics and Markov transition kernels

The following choices for the function
f
in
\mathsf{T}_{f}
in Lemmas
3.12
,
3.13
,
3.14
below are the basic building blocks for the models we study. They correspond to transition probabilities for an inhomogeneous space Bernoulli walk, geometric walk and continuous time pure-birth chain.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
f(z)=1-\alpha z
. We have, with
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)=\alpha a_{x}\mathbf{1}_{y=x+1}+(1-\alpha a_{x})\mathbf{1}_{y=x}.

(48)

Proof.

Immediate evaluation of the contour integral using the residue formula.
∎

Note that, for the above expression to be positive and thus have probabilistic meaning we need
0\leq\alpha\leq\left(\sup_{k}a_{k}\right)^{-1}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
f(z)=(1+\beta z)^{-1}
. We have, with
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)=\frac{1}{1+\beta a_{y}}\prod_{k=x}^{y-1}\frac{\beta a_{k}}{1+\beta a_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{y\geq x}.

(49)

Proof.

This is again a direct derivation but a little less trivial so we give the details. For the computation we assume that all the
a_{k}
’s are distinct and then remove this restriction by continuity. After evaluating the contour integral in terms of residues (with all the
a_{k}
’s distinct) and some relabelling, in order to prove (
49
), we are required to show that

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\prod_{j\neq k}\frac{1}{a_{j}-a_{k}}\frac{1}{1+\beta a_{k}}=\beta^{n}\prod_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{1+\beta a_{k}}.

Clearing the denominators we need to show

\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\prod_{m>l,l\neq k}(a_{m}-a_{l})\prod_{l\neq k}(1+\beta a_{l})=\beta^{n}\prod_{m>l}(a_{m}-a_{l}).

Now, observe that the left hand side can be written as the determinant

\det\begin{bmatrix}\prod_{l\neq 0}(1+\beta a_{l})&1&a_{0}&\cdots&a_{0}^{n-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\prod_{l\neq n}(1+\beta a_{l})&1&a_{n}&\cdots&a_{n}^{n-2}\end{bmatrix}.

Replace
a_{n}
by a variable
w
and consider the following polynomial of degree
n-1
in
w

\displaystyle w\mapsto\det\begin{bmatrix}\prod_{l\neq 0}^{n-1}(1+\beta a_{l})(1+\beta w)&1&a_{0}&\cdots&a_{0}^{n-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\prod_{l=0}^{n-1}(1+\beta a_{l})&1&w&\cdots&w^{n-2}\end{bmatrix}.

It has roots at
w=a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}
. So it is equal to

C_{n}^{\beta}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(w-a_{i})

where
C_{n}^{\beta}
is the coefficient of the
w^{n-1}
term. This term is obtained from

\displaystyle w^{n-2}\det\begin{bmatrix}\prod_{l\neq 0}(1+\beta a_{l})(1+\beta w)&1&a_{0}&\cdots&a_{0}^{n-2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\prod_{l\neq n-1}(1+\beta a_{l})(1+\beta w)&1&w&\cdots&w^{n-2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=w^{n-2}(1+\beta w)\det\begin{bmatrix}\prod_{l\neq 0}(1+\beta a_{l})&1&a_{0}&\cdots&a_{0}^{n-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\prod_{l\neq n-1}(1+\beta a_{l})&1&a_{n-1}&\cdots&a_{n-1}^{n-3}\end{bmatrix}.

Hence, we get

C_{n}^{\beta}=\beta\det\begin{bmatrix}\prod_{l\neq 0}(1+\beta a_{l})&1&a_{0}&\cdots&a_{0}^{n-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\prod_{l\neq n-1}(1+\beta a_{l})&1&a_{n-1}&\cdots&a_{n-1}^{n-3}\end{bmatrix}

and the claim follows by induction.
∎

Again, for the above expression for
\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)
to be positive we need
\beta\geq 0
.

Lemma 3.14
.

The probability that a pure-birth chain having jump rate
a_{k}
at location
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
goes from
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
to
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
in time
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is given by
\mathsf{T}_{f}(x,y)
with
f(z)=e^{-tz}
.

Proof.

This is well-known. One easily shows that
\mathsf{T}_{e^{-tz}}(x,y)
solves the corresponding Kolmogorov equation. A derivation can be found, for example, in
[
133
]
.
∎

4

An intertwining

4.1

Intertwining for non-intersecting paths

In this section, in a sense that will be clearer in the sequel, we prove that the dynamics on single levels of the interlacing arrays are consistent from
N
to
N+1
. We need some definitions and notation.

Definition 4.1
.

Given
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
define the following kernel
\mathsf{P}^{(N)}_{f}
on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
by

\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(50)

Definition 4.2
.

We define the following non-negative kernel
\Lambda_{N+1,N}
from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N}
by

\Lambda_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}},\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\ \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}.

(51)

We have the following alternative description of
\Lambda_{N+1,N}
using the well-known fact that
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}}
can be written as a determinant with indicator function entries
[
134
,
26
]
. To do this, we will use the standard notational device in the setting of extending the set
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
by an extra symbol that we denote by
\mathsf{virt}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
N\geq 1
. Define the function
\phi:\left(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\cup\{\mathsf{virt}\}\right)\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
by, with
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\phi(x,y)=\begin{cases}-a_{x}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{y>x},\ \ &x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\\
1,\ \ &x=\mathsf{virt}.\end{cases}

Then, with
x_{N+1}=\mathsf{virt}
we have

\Lambda_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=\det\left(\phi(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}.

(52)

We observe the composition property.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
f,g\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
with
R>R(\mathbf{a})
. Then, we have

\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}\mathsf{P}_{g}^{(N)}=\mathsf{P}_{fg}^{(N)}.

(53)

Proof.

This is a direct application of the Cauchy-Binet formula and Lemma
3.4
.
∎

For certain choices of functions
f
we obtain that
\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}
has a probabilistic interpretation, for any
N\geq 1
, in terms of non-intersecting paths. Of course, for
N=1
this is already a consequence of Lemmas
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
f(z)
be a (possibly infinite) product of factors of the form
1-\alpha_{i}z,(1+\beta_{i}z)^{-1},e^{-tz}
, where
0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq\left(\sup_{k}a_{k}\right)^{-1}
,
0\leq\beta_{i}<\infty
and
t\geq 0
, such that
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
. Then,
\mathsf{P}^{(N)}_{f}
is non-negative.

Proof.

If
f(z)=e^{-tz}
, then
\mathsf{P}^{(N)}_{f}
, by virtue of Lemma
3.14
, corresponds to the Karlin-McGregor
[
90
]
transition kernel of independent pure-birth chains killed when they intersect. If instead
f(z)
is a finite product of factors of the form
1-\alpha_{i}z,(1+\beta_{i}z)^{-1}
satisfying
0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq\left(\sup_{k}a_{k}\right)^{-1}
,
0\leq\beta_{i}<\infty
, then
\mathsf{P}^{(N)}_{f}
, by virtue of Lemma
3.12
and Lemma
3.13
is given by the Lindstrom-Gessel-Viennot (LGV) formula for non-intersecting walks on a weighted directed acyclic graph
[
69
]
, see Figure
10
for an illustration. It is the transition probability of independent walks, taking either Bernoulli or geometric steps, in discrete-time, killed when they intersect. Finally, the general product case follows from Proposition
4.4
and a limit in the number of factors.
∎

x
x+1
x+2
x+3
x+4
x
x+1
x+2
x+3
x+4
1-\alpha a_{x}
\alpha a_{x}
1-\alpha a_{x+1}
\alpha a_{x+1}
1-\alpha a_{x+2}
\alpha a_{x+2}
1-\alpha a_{x+3}
\alpha a_{x+3}
1-\alpha a_{x+4}

x
x+1
x+2
x+3
x+4
x
x+1
x+2
x+3
x+4
(1+\beta a_{x})^{-1}
\frac{\beta a_{x}}{1+\beta a_{x}}
(1+\beta a_{x+1})^{-1}
\frac{\beta a_{x+1}}{1+\beta a_{x+1}}
(1+\beta a_{x+2})^{-1}
\frac{\beta a_{x+2}}{1+\beta a_{x+2}}
(1+\beta a_{x+3})^{-1}
\frac{\beta a_{x+3}}{1+\beta a_{x+3}}
(1+\beta a_{x+4})^{-1}

Figure 10:
An illustration of part of the LGV graphs corresponding to a single Bernoulli step with parameter
\alpha
, namely corresponding to the function
f(z)=1-\alpha z
and a single geometric step with parameter
\beta
, corresponding to the function
f(z)=(1+\beta z)^{-1}
. We can join such elementary graphs one after the other.

The following intertwining relation between the transition kernels (although note that at this stage positivity is not yet required)
\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}
and
\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N+1)}
is at the heart of many of our results. We give a direct proof but different arguments for a proof will also be presented in the sequel.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
N\geq 1
. Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
, with
f(0)=1
. Then, we have the intertwining

\mathsf{P}^{(N+1)}_{f}\Lambda_{N+1,N}=\Lambda_{N+1,N}\mathsf{P}^{(N)}_{f}.

(54)

Proof.

We first compute the left hand side using Cauchy-Binet formula to obtain, where we use Lemma
3.5
and the fact that
f(0)=1
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{(N+1)}_{f}\Lambda_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f}(y_{i},z_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}\det\left(\phi(x_{i},z_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}

\displaystyle=\det\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{T}_{f}\phi^{*}(y_{1},x_{1})&\cdots&\mathsf{T}_{f}\phi^{*}(y_{1},x_{N})&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\mathsf{T}_{f}\phi^{*}(y_{N+1},x_{1})&\cdots&\mathsf{T}_{f}\phi^{*}(y_{N+1},x_{N})&1\end{bmatrix}.

Here, and below,
\phi^{*}(x,y)=\phi(y,x)
and
\phi^{*}\mathsf{T}_{f}
and
\mathsf{T}_{f}\phi^{*}
denotes convolution (not multiplication). We now compute the right hand side, by expanding along the last column and using Cauchy-Binet again

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N+1,N}\mathsf{P}^{(N)}_{f}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\det\left(\phi(z_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f}(z_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{N+1}(-1)^{N+1-l}\det\left(\phi^{*}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i=1,\dots,N+1,i\neq l;j=1,\dots,N}

\displaystyle=\det\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{*}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y_{1},x_{1})&\cdots&\phi^{*}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y_{1},x_{N})&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\phi^{*}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y_{N+1},x_{1})&\cdots&\phi^{*}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y_{N+1},x_{N})&1\end{bmatrix}.

We note that from Lemma
3.6
we have

a_{y}^{-1}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y,x)=a_{x}^{-1}\nabla_{y}^{+}\sum_{z>x}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y,z)

and thus by summing over
y
we obtain

\displaystyle\phi^{*}\mathsf{T}_{f}(y,x)=\mathsf{T}_{f}\phi^{*}(y,x)-\mathsf{T}_{f}\phi^{*}(0,x).

(55)

Now, using the identity above and column operations we obtain the desired equality.
∎

We now go on to obtain an analogous intertwining for the normalised versions of
\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}
and
\Lambda_{N+1,N}
to be defined shortly. We need the following definition.

Definition 4.7
.

For any
N\geq 1
we define the following strictly positive function
\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{a})
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
recursively by
\mathfrak{h}_{1}(x)=1
and

\mathfrak{h}_{N+1}(\mathbf{x})=\Lambda_{N+1,N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}).

Remark 4.8
.

Observe that, by comparing the definitions of the two functions we get that
\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})=\textnormal{dim}_{N}(\mathbf{x})
from Section
2.5
on the inhomogeneous Gelfand-Tsetlin graph.

From Theorem
4.6
we immediately obtain the following.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
N\geq 1
. Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
, with
f(0)=1
. Then

\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}\mathfrak{h}_{N}=\mathfrak{h}_{N}
.

Thus, by a Doob
h
-transform, see
[
61
,
125
]
, by
\mathfrak{h}_{N}
we can define the following kernels
\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}
from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N}
and from
\mathbb{W}_{N}
to itself respectively. By construction,
\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}
is Markov. Similarly, as long as
f
is so that
\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}
is non-negative, then
\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}
is also Markov.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
N\geq 1
. Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
, with
f(0)=1
. Define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(56)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N+1}(\mathbf{y})}\Lambda_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}.

(57)

Here, we are slightly abusing notation as we have already defined
\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{f}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for special choices of
f=f_{s,t}
in (
12
). By virtue of Lemma
7.10
the expressions (
12
) and (
56
) are one and the same. Observe that, the following theorem is then a direct consequence of Theorem
4.6
and the above definitions.

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
N\geq 1
. Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
, with
f(0)=1
. Then,

\mathfrak{P}^{(N+1)}_{f}\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}=\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{f}.

(58)

4.2

Intertwinings for determinantal kernels

In this subsection we introduce a slightly more general framework for intertwinings involving determinants which may be of independent interest. As we explain below, from this setup it would be possible to anticipate Theorem
4.6
, although we do not give the details required for a complete independent proof.

Fix
N\geq 1
. We are given the following data: for
1\leq j\leq N+1
, parameters
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\lambda_{j}\neq\lambda_{k}
and corresponding functions
h_{\lambda_{j}}:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
, for
i=1,2,3,4
, kernels

A^{(i)}:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\cup\{\mathsf{virt}\})\to\mathbb{C}
, such that, with the series assumed to be converging absolutely,

A^{(i)}h_{\lambda_{j}}(x)=\sum_{y=0}^{\infty}A^{(i)}(x,y)h_{\lambda_{j}}(y)=c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(i)}h_{\lambda_{j}}(x)

(59)

and moreover
A^{(i)}(x,\mathsf{virt})=h_{\lambda_{N+1}}(x)
. Finally, assume that for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
, with
1\leq n\leq N
,

\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(x_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{n}\neq 0.

Definition 4.12
.

Given the data in the above paragraph, define the kernels, for
i=1,2,3,4,

\displaystyle A_{n}^{(i)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j-1}^{n}\frac{1}{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(i)}}\frac{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(y_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{n}}{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(x_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{n}}\det\left(A^{(i)}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{n},\ \ \mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{n},\ \ n=1,\dots,N+1,

\displaystyle A_{N+1,N}^{(i)}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(i)}}\frac{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(y_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(x_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}}\det\left(A^{(i)}(y_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1},\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}.

Then, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 4.13
.

Assume the above, then we have, for
i=1,2,3,4,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{n}}A_{n}^{(i)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1,\ \ \sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}A^{(i)}_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=1,

(60)

where we write
x_{N+1}=\mathsf{virt}
. Moreover, if we assume, for
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

A^{(1)}A^{(2)}(x,y)=A^{(3)}A^{(4)}(x,y)+\tilde{h}(y)h_{\lambda_{N+1}}(x)

(61)

for some (possibly identically zero) function
\tilde{h}:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
we have the intertwining

A_{N+1}^{(1)}A^{(2)}_{N+1,N}=A^{(3)}_{N+1,N}A^{(4)}_{N}.

(62)

Proof.

In the case of
A_{n}^{(i)}
, the normalization (
60
) is a direct consequence of the Cauchy-Binet formula and the eigenfunction relation (
59
). In the case of
A_{N,N-1}^{(i)}
we expand along the last column, use Cauchy-Binet and the eigenfunction relation (
59
), from which (
60
) follows.

For the intertwining, we compute the left hand side first, with
x_{N+1}=\mathsf{virt}
using the Cauchy-Binet formula

\displaystyle\left[A_{N+1}^{(1)}A_{N+1,N}^{(2)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{N+1}\frac{1}{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(1)}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(2)}}\frac{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(x_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(y_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}}\det\left(A^{(1)}A^{(2)}(y_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}.

Now, we work on the right hand side, we expand along the last column of the size-
(N+1)
determinant, and use Cauchy-Binet to obtain

\displaystyle\left[A_{N+1,N}^{(3)}A_{N}^{(4)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{N+1}\frac{1}{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(3)}}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(4)}}\frac{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(x_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(y_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}}\det\left(A^{(3)}A^{(4)}(y_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}.

Now, observe that the last column of the determinants on the left and right hand sides involving the kernels
A^{(1)}A^{(2)}
and
A^{(3)}A^{(4)}
has entries in the
i
-th row given by

c_{\lambda_{N+1}}^{(2)}h_{\lambda_{N+1}}(x_{i})
and
c_{\lambda_{N+1}}^{(3)}h_{\lambda_{N+1}}(x_{i})
respectively. Using relation (
61
) and column operations we then obtain

\det\left(A^{(1)}A^{(2)}(y_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}=\frac{c_{\lambda_{N+1}}^{(1)}}{c_{\lambda_{N+1}}^{(3)}}\det\left(A^{(3)}A^{(4)}(y_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N+1}.

Thus, we get

\left[A_{N+1}^{(1)}A_{N+1,N}^{(2)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(3)}c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(4)}}{c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(1)}c_{\lambda_{j}}^{(2)}}\left[A_{N+1,N}^{(3)}A_{N}^{(4)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}).

Since, as we have proven earlier, both sides sum over
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
to
1
we obtain that the ratio of constants must be equal to
1
(for example, when
\tilde{h}\equiv 0
then this is trivial to see from the eigenfunction relation) and this gives the desired intertwining.
∎

Of course, for the above to have any probabilistic meaning one needs to address the non-trivial question of positivity of the various kernels.

We now explain, without giving the rigorous details, how to get Theorem
4.6
from this framework. Let
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
, with
R>R(\mathbf{a})
and
f(0)=1
, be arbitrary. Take
A^{(1)}=A^{(4)}=\mathsf{T}_{f}
. Let
g_{\lambda_{N+1}}(w)=(w-\lambda_{N+1})^{-1}
. Take

\displaystyle A^{(2)}(x,y)=A^{(3)}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{a_{y}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{p_{x}(w)g_{\lambda_{N+1}}(w)}{p_{y+1}(w)}dw.

Note that,
g_{\lambda_{N+1}}\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{\lambda_{N+1}}\right)
and observe that
A^{(2)}=A^{(3)}=\mathsf{T}_{g_{\lambda_{N+1}}}
for
\lambda_{N+1}
large and negative (since then the pole at
\lambda_{N+1}
is not contained in the contour
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}
). Take, as
h_{\lambda}(x)=p_{x}(\lambda)
which, from Lemma
3.7
, is an eigenfunction of the
A^{(i)}
with eigenvalues
c^{(1)}_{\lambda}=c^{(4)}_{\lambda}=f(\lambda)
and
c^{(2)}_{\lambda}=c_{\lambda}^{(3)}=(\lambda-\lambda_{N+1})^{-1}
. Finally, from Lemma
3.4
all the
A^{(i)}
operators commute when
\lambda_{N+1}<-R(\mathbf{a})
. We now take
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N+1}\to 0
. First, note that
h_{\lambda_{j}}(x)\to 1
and as
\lambda_{N+1}\to 0
,
A^{(2)}(x,y)=A^{(3)}(x,y)\to\phi^{*}(x,y)=\phi(y,x)
using the representation of
\phi
in Lemma
4.3
. Moreover, although the operators
A^{(i)}
no longer commute if
\lambda_{N+1}
is small (the reason is that we have an extra residue coming from the pole of
g_{\lambda_{N+1}}
at
\lambda_{N+1}
in
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}
) we still get a relation of the form (
61
) which essentially becomes (
55
) in the limit. Furthermore, ratios of functions
\det\left(h_{\lambda_{j}}(x_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{n}
combined with the eigevalues
c^{(i)}_{\lambda}
converge to ratios of functions
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
, by virtue of the representation of the
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
given in Lemma
7.10
. Putting everything together, and after some manipulations we formally obtain the intertwining in Theorem
4.6
.

5

Some couplings

In this section we introduce certain couplings between the intertwined semigroups from Section
4
that give rise to the push-block type dynamics introduced in Section
2.2
. These constructions have their origin, in some sense, in the most basic coalescing random walk model that we discuss next. The following space of two-level interlaced configurations will make its appearance often:

\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}=\left\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}\times\mathbb{W}_{N+1}:\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}\right\}.

(63)

5.1

Couplings from coalescing random walks

Suppose we are given a sequence of functions
\left(f_{t,t+1}(z)\right)_{t=0}^{\infty}
, with either
f_{t,t+1}(z)=1-\alpha_{t}z
or
f_{t,t+1}(z)=(1+\beta_{t}z)^{-1}
, where for all
t\geq 0
, the parameters satisfy
0\leq\alpha_{t}\leq(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x})^{-1}
and
0\leq\beta_{t}<\infty
. In particular,
\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}
corresponds to either an inhomogeneous Bernoulli or geometric jump. For
s\leq t
, define the function
f_{s,t}
(with
f_{t,t}=1
) by

f_{s,t}(z)=f_{s,s+1}(z)\cdots f_{t-1,t}(z).

At each space-time point
(x,t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
we put an independent Bernoulli random variable
\mathsf{U}_{x,t}
which can be one of two kinds depending on
f_{t,t+1}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{x,t}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\alpha)},\ \ &\textnormal{ if }f_{t,t+1}(z)=1-\alpha_{t}z,\\
\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\beta)},\ \ &\textnormal{ if }f_{t,t+1}(z)=(1+\beta_{t}z)^{-1},\end{cases}

where the Bernoulli variables
\mathsf{U}^{(\alpha)}_{x,t},\mathsf{U}^{(\beta)}_{x,t}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\alpha)}=1\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\alpha)}=0\right)=\alpha_{t}a_{x},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\beta)}=1\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\beta)}=0\right)=\frac{\beta_{t}a_{x}}{1+\beta_{t}a_{x}}.

Then, we do the following:

•

If
\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\alpha)}=1
we put an arrow going from
(x,t)
to
(x+1,t+1)
.

•

If
\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\alpha)}=0
we put an arrow going from
(x,t)
to
(x,t+1)
.

•

If
\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\beta)}=1
we put an arrow going from
(x,t)
to
(x+1,t)
.

•

If
\mathsf{U}_{x,t}^{(\beta)}=0
we put an arrow going from
(x,t)
to
(x,t+1)
.

Note that, every space-time point has exactly one outgoing arrow but possibly multiple incoming arrows. See Figure
11
for an illustration.

Definition 5.1
.

For any times
s\leq t
, we define the random map
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}:\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{Z}_{+}
as follows:
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x)
is the location at time
t
of the (deterministic) motion starting from
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
at time
s
which follows the random arrows in the above construction.

See Figure
11
for an illustration of
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}
.

x
x+1
x+2
x+3
t=0
t=1
t=2
t=3

Figure 11:
An illustration of the construction of
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}
. The arrows are random constructed from the independent random variables
\mathsf{U}_{x,t}
at each space-time point
(x,t)
as explained in the text. The first step from time
0
to time
1
is a Bernoulli step, the second step is a geometric step and the third step is again a Bernoulli step. The random walks starting at
x
and
x+1
move independently for one step and then they coalesce and move together. For example,
\mathcal{Z}_{0,1}(x)=\mathcal{Z}_{0,1}(x+1)=x+1
,
\mathcal{Z}_{0,2}(x)=\mathcal{Z}_{0,2}(x+1)=x+1
,
\mathcal{Z}_{0,3}(x)=\mathcal{Z}_{0,3}(x+1)=x+2
.

Observe that, by construction, almost surely for all
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{3}
we have

\mathcal{Z}_{t_{2},t_{3}}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(x)\right)=\mathcal{Z}_{t_{1},t_{3}}(x),\ \ \forall x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

We can thus think of
\left(\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}\right)_{s\leq t}
as a stochastic flow of maps. Fixing the starting time
s
and locations
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
the following process is called the
N
-point motion of the flow

t\mapsto\left(\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{N})\right),\ t\geq s.

By its very construction this motion is distributed as
N
random walks starting from locations
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
at time
s
, moving independently with Bernoulli or geometric jumps (depending on the corresponding functions
f_{u,u+1}
for
u\geq s
) until any two walks meet at a space-time point
(x,t)
from which time onwards they move together (as the same random variables are used to drive both their evolutions).

We can compute the distribution of this process explicitly.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
s\leq t
. Let
x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}
and
y_{1}\leq\cdots\leq y_{N}
. Then,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{1})\leq y_{1},\dots,\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{N})\leq y_{N}\right)=\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}\rrbracket}(x_{i})-\mathbf{1}_{i<j}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(64)

Proof.

We first assume
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
and then remove this restriction below. Observe that by summing over the LGV-formula we obtain

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{1})\leq y_{1}<\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{2})\leq y_{2}<\cdots<\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{N})\leq y_{N}\right)=\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}\rrbracket}(x_{i})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

Observe that this formula would have simply been zero and thus not immediately useful if the
x_{i}
were not distinct. Then, by writing the indicator

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{1})\leq y_{1},\dots,\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{N})\leq y_{N}}

in terms of indicators

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{i_{1}})\leq y_{j_{1}}<\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{i_{2}})\leq y_{i_{2}}<\cdots<\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{i_{k}})\leq y_{j_{k}}}

for increasing sequences
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
and
j_{1},\dots,j_{k}
as explained in detail in Proposition 9 of
[
134
]
we obtain the desired formula for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
. We now explain how to remove the restriction that the coordinates of
\mathbf{x}
are distinct. Suppose we have
m
distinct values

\displaystyle x_{1}=\cdots=x_{i_{1}-1},x_{i_{1}}=\cdots=x_{i_{2}-1},\dots,x_{i_{m-1}}=\cdots=x_{N}.

By the coalescence property we have, with
i_{0}=1
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{i})\leq y_{i},\ \ \textnormal{ for }1\leq i\leq N\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{i_{j-1}})\leq y_{i_{j-1}},\ \ \textnormal{ for }1\leq j\leq m\right).

Thus, we need to show that the size
N
determinant on the right hand side of (
64
) boils down to the corresponding size
m
determinant. Using the special structure of the determinant this is easy to prove. For
j=0,\dots,m-1
subtract row
i_{j}
from rows
i_{j}+1,\dots,i_{j+1}-1
. Then, by swapping consecutive rows bring rows
i_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}
to positions
1,\dots,m
and similarly by swapping consecutive columns bring columns
i_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}
to positions
1,\dots,m
. We then get the determinant of a block triangular matrix whose top-left corner is the
m\times m
matrix of interest and whose bottom-right corner is the identity matrix. The conclusion follows.
∎

The following definition is central in our argument.

Definition 5.3
.

Consider the following kernel, defined as the
(2N+1)\times(2N+1)
determinant, with
s\leq t
and
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\det\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{A}_{s,t}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})&\mathcal{B}_{s,t}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\\
\mathcal{C}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})&\mathcal{D}_{s,t}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\end{bmatrix},

where the entries of the matrices
\mathcal{A}_{s,t}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{B}_{s,t}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{C}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{D}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
of sizes
(N+1)\times(N+1)
,
(N+1)\times N
,
N\times(N+1)
and
N\times N
respectively are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{s,t}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{y_{j}^{\prime}}^{-}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i})=\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}(y_{i},y_{j}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{s,t}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{x_{j}^{\prime}}}\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i})-\mathbf{1}_{j\geq i}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=a_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x_{i}}^{+}\nabla_{y_{j}^{\prime}}^{-}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(x_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a_{x_{i}}}{a_{x_{j}^{\prime}}}\nabla_{x_{i}}^{+}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(x_{i})=\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime}).

Remark 5.4
.

One could replace the function
f_{s,t}
in the definition above by a general
f\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-\epsilon}\right)
for some
\epsilon>0
to define a kernel
\mathcal{Q}_{f}^{N,N+1}
. Then, from an analytic standpoint the results in Propositions
5.7
and
5.9
extend to this setting as well. What is not clear however is whether they have any probabilistic meaning for more general
f
.

Proposition 5.5
.

We have, where
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{a_{x_{i}}}{a_{x_{i}^{\prime}}}(-\nabla_{x_{i}}^{+})\prod_{i=1}^{N+1}(-\nabla_{y_{i}^{\prime}}^{-})\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(y_{i})\leq y_{i}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{j})\leq x_{j}^{\prime},\textnormal{ for all }i,j\right).

Proof.

This follows by virtue of Proposition
5.2
and the explicit Definition
5.3
of
\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}
and careful inspection of the two formulae.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
s\leq t
. Viewed as a kernel from
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
to itself,
\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{s,t}
is sub-Markov:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\geq 0,\ \ \forall(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{w}_{N,N+1}}\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1,\ \ \forall(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}.

Proof.

Proving non-negativity directly from the determinant formula is actually tricky. We use the connection to the flow
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}
via Proposition
5.5
instead. Observe that, by the very construction of the coalescing flow
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}
the events

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(x_{i})\leq x_{i}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}(y_{i})\leq y_{j}^{\prime},\ \textnormal{ for all }i,j\right\}

are increasing as the variables
x_{i}
decrease and the variables
y_{j}^{\prime}
increase. The claim then follows from the representation given in Proposition
5.5
of
\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{s,t}
in terms of the probability of such events.

We now move to the second item of the statement. We show that

\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}:(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}}\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\det\left(\mathcal{D}_{s,t}(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}=\mathsf{P}^{(N)}_{f_{s,t}}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right).

Note that, this is nothing else but the LGV formula
[
69
]
for non-intersecting paths discussed in Proposition
4.5
so it is sub-Markov and hence the sum over
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{s,t}
is indeed less than
1
. The above claim can be seen in two ways (which are essentially equivalent). First, by direct computation. Use multinearity to bring the sum over
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
inside the determinant definition of
\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{s,t}
and then use the relations (we use the convention
x_{N+1}^{\prime}\equiv\infty
and
x_{0}^{\prime}\equiv-1
)

\displaystyle\sum_{y_{j}^{\prime}=x_{j-1}^{\prime}+1}^{x_{j}^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}_{s,t}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i})-\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j-1}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i}),

\displaystyle\sum_{y_{j}^{\prime}=x_{j-1}^{\prime}+1}^{x_{j}^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=-a_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x_{i}}^{+}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(x_{i})+a_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x_{i}}^{+}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j-1}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(x_{i}).

By some simple row-column operations the claim follows. Second, using the flow
representation in Proposition
5.5
take the sum over
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
to get rid of the discrete derivatives in
y_{j}^{\prime}
, then use the formula in Proposition
5.2
and conclude by virtue of Lemma
3.6
.
∎

A more involved argument, that introduces an inverse
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}^{-1}
to the flow
\mathcal{Z}_{s,t}
can be used to establish the semigroup property
\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{t_{1},t_{2}}\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{t_{2},t_{3}}=\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{t_{1},t_{3}}
, for
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{3}
. The actual dynamics of the induced process on
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
, as far as we can tell, cannot be obtained directly using this connection and we need to take a different approach in the next subsections. We believe that
\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}
should correspond to the transition probabilities of two-level dynamics taking sequential-update Bernoulli steps or Warren-Windridge geometric steps of certain parameters depending on whether each factor
f_{u,u+1}(z)
is given by
(1-\alpha_{u}z)
or
(1+\beta_{u}z)^{-1}
. We will prove this in the case of Bernoulli jumps in Proposition
5.10
and take yet another approach to consider geometric jumps in Section
5.3
.

Before discussing such dynamics further we prove a number of intertwining relations (which in fact hold beyond the probabilistic setting, see Remark
5.4
). Towards this end, define the projection
\Pi_{N}:\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}\to\mathbb{W}_{N}
by projecting on the
\mathbf{x}
-coordinate. In particular, for a function
g
on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
we thus define a function
\Pi_{N}g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=g(\mathbf{x})
on
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
s\leq t
. We have

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\Pi_{N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Pi_{N}\mathsf{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right),\

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(65)

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N+1)}\Lambda_{N+1,N}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Lambda_{N+1,N}\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right),\

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1},\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},

(66)

where we view
\Lambda_{N+1,N}
given in Definition
4.2
as a kernel from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
in the obvious way

\Lambda_{N+1,N}\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{k})\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{y}}.

Proof.

The equality (
65
) is shown using the computation at the end of the proof of Proposition
5.6
. For equality (
66
), we need to sum over
\mathbf{x}
such that
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}
. We use multinearity to bring the sum inside the determinant and the relations

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}={y_{i}}}^{y_{i+1}-1}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}}\mathcal{C}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{y_{j}^{\prime}}^{+}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i+1})-\nabla_{y_{j}^{\prime}}^{+}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i}),

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}={y_{i}}}^{y_{i+1}-1}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}}\mathcal{D}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{a_{x_{j}^{\prime}}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i+1})+\frac{1}{a_{x_{j}}^{\prime}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i}).

Then, the statement follows from simple row-column operations.
∎

Observe that, by a combination of displays (
65
) and (
66
) we obtain yet another proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
s\leq t
. The kernel defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}^{N,N+1}_{s,t}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{s,t}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]

(67)

is a Markov kernel from
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
to itself.

Proof.

By combining Propositions
4.9
and
5.7
we obtain

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{s,t}\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}\right](\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),

from which the result follows.
∎

If by abusing notation we view
\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}
as a Markov kernel from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
as we did with
\Lambda_{N+1,N}
, by combining the results above we readily obtain the intertwinings of Markov kernels.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
s\leq t
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\Pi_{N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right),\

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(68)

\displaystyle\left[\mathfrak{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N+1)}\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathsf{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right),\

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1},\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}.

(69)

5.2

Sequential-update Bernoulli coupling

In this section we prove that the formula for
\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}
given in Definition
5.3
with
f_{u,u+1}(z)=1-\alpha_{u}z
, where
0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq\left(\sup_{k}a_{k}\right)^{-1}
, actually corresponds to the sequential-update Bernoulli push-block dynamics. More precisely, we have the following result. Observe that, in the definition of the stochastic process
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t\geq 0\right)
below the interaction of the
\mathsf{Y}
-components with the
\mathsf{X}
-components is exactly given by the interaction between consecutive levels in the sequential-update Bernoulli dynamics of Definition
2.5
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Consider the following stochastic process
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t\geq 0\right)
in
\mathbb{W}_{N}\times\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
in discrete time initialised at
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
. The component
\left(\mathsf{X}(t);t\geq 0\right)
evolves autonomously as
N
independent Bernoulli walks, each with transition probabilities from time
s
to time
s+1
given by
\mathsf{T}_{(1-\alpha_{s}z)}
. Then, given the updated component
\mathsf{X}(s+1)
we update
\mathsf{Y}(s)
to
\mathsf{Y}(s+1)
as follows:

•

If
\mathsf{Y}_{i}(s)=x
and
\mathsf{X}_{i}(s+1)=x
then
\mathsf{Y}_{i}(s+1)=x
(block).

•

If
\mathsf{Y}_{i}(s)=x
and
\mathsf{X}_{i-1}(s+1)=x
then
\mathsf{Y}_{i}(s+1)=x+1
(push).

•

Otherwise,
\mathsf{Y}_{i}
moves as a Bernoulli random walk with transition probability
\mathsf{T}_{(1-\alpha_{s}z)}
independent of the other coordinates
\mathsf{Y}_{j}
.

The whole process is killed at the first collision time
\tau=\inf\{t\geq 0:\mathsf{X}(t)\notin\mathbb{W}_{N}\}
of the
\mathsf{X}
coordinates, so that in particular
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t)\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
for all
t<\tau
. Then, the transition probabilities from time
s
to time
t
of
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t\geq 0\right)
killed when it exits
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
are given by
\mathcal{Q}_{s,t}^{N,N+1}
with the underlying functions given by
f_{i,i+1}(z)=1-\alpha_{i}z
, where
0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq\left(\sup_{k}a_{k}\right)^{-1}
.

Proof.

Take a test function
g:\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Fix an arbitrary time
T\geq 0
. Define for any
0\leq t\leq T
, the functions (we drop dependence on
N
)

\displaystyle F\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}}\mathcal{Q}^{N,N+1}_{t,T}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]g(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle G\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[g\left(\mathsf{X}(T),\mathsf{Y}(T)\right)\mathbf{1}_{T<\tau}\big|\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t)\right)=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),t\leq\tau\right].

We now show, by backward induction in
t
, that
F
and
G
are equal from which the proposition follows. Observe that,

F\left(T,T,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)=G\left(T,T,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)=g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\ \ (\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}.

Then, observe that by the way our dynamics work
G
satisfies the following set of equations (the last three equations (
71
), (
72
), (
73
) define the value of
G\left(t+1,T,\left(\mathbf{x}+\boldsymbol{\delta},\mathbf{y}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right)\right)
in (
70
) below when
(\mathbf{x}+\boldsymbol{\delta},\mathbf{y}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon})\notin\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
), with the first equation holding for
0\leq t\leq T-1
and the rest for
0\leq t\leq T
:

\displaystyle G\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)=\sum_{\delta_{i},\epsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}(x_{i},x_{i}+\delta_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{N+1}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}(y_{i},y_{i}+\epsilon_{i})G\left(t+1,T,\left(\mathbf{x}+\boldsymbol{\delta},\mathbf{y}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right)\right),

(70)

\displaystyle\nabla_{y_{i}}^{+}G\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)\big|_{y_{i}=x_{i}}=0\ \ (\textnormal{i-th particle is blocked}),

(71)

\displaystyle\nabla_{y_{i+1}}^{+}G\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)\big|_{y_{i+1}=x_{i}}=0\ \ (\textnormal{(i+1)-th particle is pushed}),

(72)

\displaystyle G\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)\big|_{x_{i}=x_{i+1}}=0\ \ (\textnormal{process killed at }\tau).

(73)

These determine
G(t,T,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))
starting from
G\left(T,T,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)=g(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. We now show that
F(t,T,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))
satisfies the same equations which completes the proof.

First, it readily follows from the form of the entries
\mathcal{C}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})_{ij}
and
\mathcal{D}_{s,t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})_{ij}
that, for
0\leq t\leq T
:

F\left(t,T,(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)\big|_{x_{i}=x_{i+1}}=0.

The boundary condition

\nabla_{y_{i}}^{+}F\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)\big|_{y_{i}=x_{i}}=0,

follows from the relations

\displaystyle\nabla_{y_{i}}^{+}\mathcal{A}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}\big|_{y_{i}=x_{i}}

\displaystyle=-a_{x_{i}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{ij},

\displaystyle\nabla_{y_{i}}^{+}\mathcal{B}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}\big|_{y_{i}=x_{i}}

\displaystyle=-a_{x_{i}}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{ij},

while the boundary condition

\nabla_{y_{i+1}}^{+}F\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)\big|_{y_{i+1}=x_{i}}=0,

follows from the relations

\displaystyle\nabla_{y_{i+1}}^{+}\mathcal{A}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{i+1j}\big|_{y_{i+1}=x_{i}}

\displaystyle=-a_{x_{i}}^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{ij},

\displaystyle\nabla_{y_{i+1}}^{+}\mathcal{B}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{i+1j}\big|_{y_{i+1}=x_{i}}

\displaystyle=-a_{x_{i}}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}.

Finally, the equation

F\left(t,T,\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\right)=\sum_{\delta_{i},\epsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}(x_{i},x_{i}+\delta_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{N+1}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}(y_{i},y_{i}+\epsilon_{i})F\left(t+1,T,\left(\mathbf{x}+\boldsymbol{\delta},\mathbf{y}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right)\right)

follows from multinearity of the determinant and the following set of equations for the individual entries

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}\left(y_{i},y_{i}+\epsilon\right)\mathcal{A}_{t+1,T}\left(\mathbf{y}+\epsilon,\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{ij},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}\left(x_{i},x_{i}+\epsilon\right)\mathcal{D}_{t+1,T}\left(\mathbf{x}+\epsilon,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{ij},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=a_{x_{j}^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,T}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i})-\mathbf{1}_{j\geq i}\right)

\displaystyle=a_{x_{j}^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}(y_{i},y_{i}+\epsilon)\mathsf{T}_{f_{t+1,T}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,x_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(y_{i})-\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}(y_{i},y_{i}+\epsilon)\mathbf{1}_{j\geq i}\right)
