\displaystyle=\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}(y_{i},y_{i}+\epsilon)\mathcal{B}_{t+1,T}\left(\mathbf{y}+\epsilon,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)_{ij},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{t,T}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=a_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x_{i}}^{+}\nabla_{y_{j}}^{-}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,T}}\mathbf{1}_{\llbracket 0,y_{j}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(x_{i})=-\nabla_{y_{j}^{\prime}}^{-}\left[a_{y_{j}^{\prime}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,T}}\left(x_{i},y_{j}^{\prime}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{y_{j}^{\prime}}^{-}\left[a_{y_{j}^{\prime}}\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}\left(x_{i},x_{i}+\epsilon\right)\mathsf{T}_{f_{t+1,T}}\left(x_{i}+\epsilon,y_{j}^{\prime}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t,t+1}}\left(x_{i},x_{i}+\epsilon\right)\mathcal{C}_{t+1,T}\left(\mathbf{x}+\epsilon,\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)_{ij}.

This completes the proof.
∎

5.3

Warren-Windridge geometric dynamics
coupling

In section we take a different approach to study the transition probabilities of the Warren-Windridge geometric dynamics. We take
f_{0,1}(z)=(1+\beta z)^{-1}
. To ease notation we write
\mathsf{T}(x,y)
for
\mathsf{T}_{f_{0,1}}(x,y)
and
\mathsf{P}^{(N)}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}
for
\mathsf{P}_{f_{0,1}}^{(N)}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{f_{0,1}}^{(N)}
respectively.

Definition 5.11
.

We write
\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]
for the transition probabilities of the following single-step discrete-time stochastic process
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t=0,1\right)
in
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
. The
\mathsf{X}
-component moves autonomously, with
\mathsf{X}(1)
given
\mathsf{X}(0)=\mathbf{x}
distributed as
\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}\left(\mathsf{x},\cdot\right)
. Given the updated component
\mathsf{X}(1)
, we update component
\mathsf{Y}
as follows:

•

Coordinate
\mathsf{Y}_{i}
is first moved to the intermediate position
\max\{\mathsf{Y}_{i}(0),\mathsf{X}_{i-1}(1)+1\}
(push).

•

\mathsf{Y}_{i}
subsequently attempts to jump to
z\geq\max\{\mathsf{Y}_{i}(0),\mathsf{X}_{i-1}(1)+1\}
with the inhomogeneous geometric probability
\mathsf{T}(\max\{\mathsf{Y}_{i}(0),\mathsf{X}_{i-1}(1)+1\},z)
.

•

All jumps to
z\geq\mathsf{X}_{i}(0)=x_{i}
are suppressed and in such case
\mathsf{Y}_{i}(1)
is taken to be
x_{i}
(block).

Remark 5.12
.

Of course, the process
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t=0,1\right)
can be extended to all times
t\geq 0
. For the
s
-th step we simply replace
f_{0,1}(z)
by
f_{s,s+1}(z)=(1+\beta_{s}z)^{-1}
and all other quantities are defined analogously using
f_{s,s+1}
. All the results that follow go through with the obvious notational modification.

Observe that, in the definition of the stochastic process
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t=0,1\right)
above the interaction of the
\mathsf{Y}
-components with the
\mathsf{X}
-components is exactly given by the interaction between consecutive levels in the Warren-Windridge geometric dynamics of Definition
2.7
. As mentioned previously we believe
17
17

17

We have shown this for
N=1
by tediously checking all possibilities.
that
\mathsf{Q}_{0,1}^{N,N+1}
, with
f_{0,1}(z)=(1+\beta z)^{-1}
, should correspond to Warren-Windridge geometric dynamics step with parameter
\beta
. In particular, we believe that the question mark on the equality below should be removed

\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]\overset{?}{=}\mathsf{Q}_{0,1}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right].

(74)

However, when geometric jumps are involved, a proof along the lines of the one given for Proposition
5.10
above turns out to be tricky. So we take a different route inspired by the work of Warren-Windridge
[
135
]
in the homogeneous case. Observe that, we have

\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathcal{R}^{N,N+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

where we write

\mathcal{R}^{N,N+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Y}(1)=\mathbf{y}^{\prime}|\mathsf{X}(1)=\mathbf{x},\mathsf{X}(0)=\mathbf{x},\mathsf{Y}(0)=\mathbf{y}\right).

Our starting point is the following explicit expression for
\mathcal{R}^{N,N+1}
.

Proposition 5.13
.

We have, with
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{N,N++1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathsf{Blk}\left(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{1},x_{1}\right)\prod_{n=2}^{N}\mathsf{b}\left(x_{n},y_{n}^{\prime}\right)\mathsf{Psh}\left(y_{n}^{\prime},y_{n},x_{n-1}^{\prime}\right)\mathsf{Psh}\left(y_{N+1}^{\prime},y_{N+1},x_{N}^{\prime}\right),

where the factors are defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Blk}\left(y^{\prime},y,x\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}(y,y^{\prime})\mathsf{b}\left(x,y^{\prime}\right),

\displaystyle\mathsf{Psh}\left(y^{\prime},y,x^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}\left(y,y^{\prime}\right)\mathbf{1}_{x^{\prime}<y}+\mathsf{T}\left(x^{\prime}+1,y^{\prime}\right)\mathbf{1}_{x^{\prime}\geq y},

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}\left(x,y^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{y^{\prime}<x}+(1+\beta a_{y^{\prime}})\mathbf{1}_{y^{\prime}=x}.

Proof.

The
\mathsf{Psh}
factor is a direct translation in symbols of the pushing mechanism. The fact that the
\mathsf{Blk}
factor corresponds to blocking can be seen by multiplying out
\mathsf{T}(y,y^{\prime})
with
\mathsf{b}(x,y^{\prime})

and using the identity, with
y\geq x
,

\sum_{m=x}^{\infty}\mathsf{T}(y,m)=\mathsf{T}(y,x)(1+\beta a_{x}).

This follows from the trivial to check identity, for
y\leq x\leq m
,

\frac{\mathsf{T}(y,m)}{\mathsf{T}(y,x)}=(1+\beta a_{x})\mathsf{T}(x,m).

Similarly, the fact that the factor with subscript
n
in the product corresponds to the interaction of
\mathsf{Y}_{n}
with
\mathsf{X}_{n-1}(1)
by pushing and
\mathsf{X}_{n}(0)
by blocking can be seen by multiplying out
\mathsf{Psh}\left(y^{\prime},y,x^{\prime}\right)
with
\mathsf{b}\left(x,y^{\prime}\right)

and the relation

\displaystyle\sum_{m=x}^{\infty}\mathsf{Psh}\left(m,y,x^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Psh}\left(x,y,x^{\prime}\right)(1+\beta a_{x}),

which is again a consequence of the previous identity.
∎

We now go on to prove an analogue of Proposition
5.9
with
\mathsf{Q}_{0,1}^{N,N+1}
replaced by
\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}
(clearly, if we knew equality (
74
) were true there would be nothing to show). The following identity is the key ingredient in the computation.

Proposition 5.14
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}=y_{i}^{\prime}}^{x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1)}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}}\mathsf{T}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\mathsf{Blk}(y_{i}^{\prime},y_{i},x_{i})

\displaystyle\mathsf{Psh}(y_{i+1}^{\prime},y_{i+1},x_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}^{\prime}}\mathsf{T}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\mathsf{T}(y_{i+1},y_{i+1}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{y_{i}\leq y_{i}^{\prime}<y_{i+1}\leq y_{i+1}^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{y_{i}^{\prime}\leq x_{i}^{\prime}<y_{i+1}^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Observe that, the left hand side is zero if we do not have
y_{i}^{\prime}<y_{i+1}
or if we do not have
y_{i}^{\prime}\leq x_{i}^{\prime}<y_{i+1}^{\prime}
. Thus, we can restrict to this scenario in which case the indicator functions on the right are both
1
. By using
\mathsf{Blk}(y_{i}^{\prime},y_{i},x_{i})=\mathsf{T}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})\mathsf{b}(x_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})
and cancelling out
\mathsf{T}(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime})
it will suffice to establish the following claim

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{i}=y_{i}^{\prime}}^{x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1)}\frac{a_{x_{i}^{\prime}}}{a_{x_{i}}}\mathsf{T}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\left(\mathbf{1}_{y_{i}^{\prime}<x_{i}}+(1+\beta a_{y_{i}^{\prime}})\mathbf{1}_{x_{i}=y_{i}^{\prime}}\right)\left(\mathsf{T}(y_{i+1},y_{i+1}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{x_{i}^{\prime}<y_{i+1}}+\mathsf{T}(x_{i}^{\prime}+1,y_{i+1}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{x_{i}^{\prime}\geq y_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{T}(y_{i+1},y_{i+1}^{\prime}).

Let us denote by
\mathcal{E}(y_{i}^{\prime})
the left hand side above as a function of
y_{i}^{\prime}
. We will now show that
\mathcal{E}(x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1))=\mathsf{T}(y_{i+1},y_{i+1}^{\prime})
and that
\mathcal{E}(m)=\mathcal{E}(m-1)
from which the claim follows.

Suppose
x_{i}^{\prime}<y_{i+1}
then
x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1)
. It is immediate then that
\mathcal{E}(x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1))=\mathsf{T}(y_{i+1},y_{i+1}^{\prime})
. If instead we suppose
x_{i}^{\prime}\geq y_{i+1}
then
x_{i}=y_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i+1}-1
. After some elementary manipulations using the explicit formulae we again obtain
\mathcal{E}(x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1))=\mathsf{T}(y_{i+1},y_{i+1}^{\prime})
. We now show
\mathcal{E}(m)=\mathcal{E}(m-1)
. We observe that the sums
\sum_{x_{i}=m+1}^{x_{i}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1)}
on both sides of the desired identity
\mathcal{E}(m)=\mathcal{E}(m-1)
are equal (the summands are exactly equal). We thus need to check that the sum
\sum_{x_{i}=m}^{m}
on the left hand side and the sum
\sum_{x_{i}=m-1}^{m}
are equal. After some manipultions this boils down to proving the identity

\frac{1}{a_{m}}\mathsf{T}(m,x_{i}^{\prime})(1+\beta a_{m})=\frac{1}{a_{m}}\mathsf{T}(m,x_{i}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{a_{m-1}}\mathsf{T}(m-1,x_{i}^{\prime})(1+\beta a_{m-1}).

Elementary manipulations readily establish this.
∎

We finally prove the required intertwinings.

Proposition 5.15
.

We have the intertwinings

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}\Pi_{N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right),\

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(75)

\displaystyle\left[\mathfrak{P}^{(N+1)}\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right),\

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1},\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}.

(76)

Proof.

The proof of (
75
) is immediate from the definition of
\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}
. For identity
(
76
) we make repeated use of Proposition
5.14
. The right hand side of (
76
) is equal to

\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N+1}(\mathbf{y})}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{T}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}^{N,N+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime};\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}).

Now, recall that

\mathsf{P}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\det\left(\mathsf{T}(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})\right)_{i=1}^{N}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{T}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}.

Moreover, we can restrict the sum above to a sum over
x_{i}\in\llbracket y_{i}^{\prime},x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1)\rrbracket
for otherwise the summand is zero and use the form of
\mathcal{R}^{N,N+1}
from Proposition
5.13
to obtain that the right hand side of (
76
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N+1}(\mathbf{y})}\sum_{x_{i}\in\llbracket y_{i}^{\prime},x_{i}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{i+1}-1)\rrbracket}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{T}(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\mathsf{Blk}\left(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{1},x_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{n=2}^{N}\mathsf{b}\left(x_{n},y_{n}^{\prime}\right)\mathsf{Psh}\left(y_{n}^{\prime},y_{n},x_{n-1}^{\prime}\right)\mathsf{Psh}\left(y_{N+1}^{\prime},y_{N+1},x_{N}^{\prime}\right).

Consider the factor

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{x_{1}}}\mathsf{T}(x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})\mathsf{Blk}(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{1},x_{1})\mathsf{Psh}(y_{2}^{\prime},y_{2},x_{1}^{\prime}).

Summing over
x_{1}\in\llbracket y_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{2}-1)\rrbracket
using Proposition
5.14
we get that this is equal to

\frac{1}{a_{x_{1}}^{\prime}}\mathsf{T}(y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})\mathsf{T}(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})\mathbf{1}_{y_{1}\leq y_{1}^{\prime}<y_{2}\leq y_{2}^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{y_{1}^{\prime}\leq x_{1}^{\prime}<y_{2}^{\prime}}.

We can then combine the factor
\mathsf{T}(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})
with
\mathsf{b}(x_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})
to get
\mathsf{Blk}(y_{2}^{\prime},y_{2},x_{2})
and perform the sum over
x_{2}\in\llbracket y_{2}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\wedge(y_{3}-1)\rrbracket
using Proposition
5.14
. If we keep iterating this procedure of using Proposition
5.14
we obtain that the right hand side of (
76
) is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N+1}(\mathbf{y})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{a_{x_{i}^{\prime}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N+1}\mathsf{T}\left(y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{y}\prec\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\prec\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}=\left[\mathfrak{P}^{(N+1)}\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right),

which concludes the proof.
∎

5.4

Continuous-time pure-birth chain coupling

We have the following continuous-time analogue for pure-birth chains of our previous discrete-time results. This result was proven in
[
8
]
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Consider the continuous-time stochastic process
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t\geq 0\right)
in
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
. The
\mathsf{X}
-component evolves autonomously according to the semigroup
\left(\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{\exp(-tz)}\right)_{t\geq 0}
.
The
\mathsf{Y}
-component evolves as
N+1
independent pure-birth chain with jump rate
a_{x}
at
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with the following interactions with the
\mathsf{X}
-component:

•

If the clock of
\mathsf{Y}_{i}
rings for it to jump and
\mathsf{Y}_{i}=\mathsf{X}_{i}
then nothing happens (block).

•

If
\mathsf{X}_{i-1}=x
and
\mathsf{Y}_{i}=x+1
and
\mathsf{X}_{i-1}
jumps to
x+1
then
\mathsf{Y}_{i}
instantaneously moves to
x+2
(push).

Then, the time-homogeneous transition probabilities of
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t\geq 0\right)
from
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
to
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
in time
t
are given by:

\mathsf{Q}_{\exp(-tz)}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\mathcal{Q}_{\exp(-tz)}^{N,N+1}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right],

(77)

where
\mathcal{Q}_{f}^{N,N+1}
is defined as in Definition
5.3
and Remark
5.4
. Moreover, we have the intertwinings:

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{Q}_{\exp(-tz)}^{N,N+1}\Pi_{N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{P}_{\exp(-tz)}^{(N)}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right),\

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(78)

\displaystyle\left[\mathfrak{P}_{\exp(-tz)}^{(N+1)}\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathsf{Q}_{\exp(-tz)}^{N,N+1}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right),\

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1},\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}.

(79)

Remark 5.17
.

Observe that, in the definition of the stochastic process
\left(\mathsf{X}(t),\mathsf{Y}(t);t\geq 0\right)
above the interaction of the
\mathsf{Y}
-components with the
\mathsf{X}
-components is exactly given by the interaction between consecutive levels in the pure-birth push-block dynamics of Definition
2.4
.

Remark 5.18
.

An analogous result holds for
\mathcal{Q}_{\exp(-tz)}^{N,N+1}
without the Doob-transform by
\mathfrak{h}_{N}
, except that now the autonomous
\mathsf{X}
-component evolves as
N
independent pure-birth chains killed when they collide (the whole process is killed as well).

5.5

Couplings of Borodin-Ferrari-Olshanski

Given two Markov semigroups which are intertwined such as
\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N+1)}
(with an appropriate choice of
f
) there are different interesting ways to couple the corresponding stochastic processes. We now briefly survey, and compare to our setting, couplings developed
18
18

18

We note that in these constructions the intertwining from Proposition
4.11
between
\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N+1)}
is taken as input. In the coalescing random walk framework the intertwining can be arrived at without apriori knowledge, see for example the discussion before Proposition
5.8
.
by Borodin, Ferrari and Olshanski which have been made use of in many works
[
24
,
20
,
32
,
21
]
, see also
[
35
,
44
,
43
]
for further developments and generalisations. Although the origin
19
19

19

These couplings have their origin in an idea of Diaconis and Fill
[
59
]
from their study of strong stationary times
[
59
,
67
,
107
,
108
]
for convergence to equilibrium for Markov chains.
of these constructions is very different from the coalescing random walk framework we have developed, it turns out that these couplings in the case of pure-birth and Bernoulli dynamics coincide with ours. On the other hand, in the case of geometric jumps their coupling is different from the Warren-Windridge geometric dynamics, which are in some sense preferable since they have more Markovian projections.

5.5.1
Sequential-update

Consider the Markov kernel, see
[
24
]
,

\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N,N+1),\textnormal{Seq}}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N+1)}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{\left[\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})},&\left[\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})>0,\\
0,&\textnormal{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(80)

on the state space

\mathbb{S}^{N,N+1}_{\textnormal{Seq}}=\left\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}\times\mathbb{W}_{N+1}\big|\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})>0\right\}=\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}.

By virtue of the intertwining from Theorem
4.11
it is immediate that this is correctly normalised. Moreover, the conditional distribution of the projection on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
given the projection on
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
is given by
\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}
, see
[
24
]
. It can be checked that in the case of Bernoulli jumps, namely with
f(z)=1-\alpha z
, the dynamics arising from (
80
) are exactly the sequential-update push-block Bernoulli dynamics we have been considering. In particular, (
80
) with
f(z)=1-\alpha z
is equal to (
67
) with
s=0,t=1
and
f_{0,1}(z)=f(z)
. In the case of geometric jumps however, the coupling obtained from (
80
) is different from the Warren-Windridge type coupling we have been considering in this work. It can be checked that its projection on the right edge is still the geometric push-TASEP but the projection on the left edge is not even Markovian (and the evolution of the rest of the array is indeed different).

5.5.2
Parallel-update

Consider the Markov kernel, see
[
24
]
,

\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N,N+1),\textnormal{Par}}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]=\frac{\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N+1)}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},\mathbf{x})}{\left[\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})}

(81)

on the more complicated state space:

\mathbb{S}^{N,N+1}_{\textnormal{Par}}=\left\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}\times\mathbb{W}_{N+1}\big|\left[\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}\right](\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})>0\right\}.

Again, by virtue of the intertwining from Theorem
4.11
it is immediate that this correctly normalised. Also, the conditional distribution of the projection on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
given the projection on
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
is now given by
\mathfrak{L}_{N+1,N}\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}
, see
[
24
]
. In the case of Bernoulli jumps this gives rise, as the projection on the left edge, to parallel-update Bernoulli TASEP. It would be possible to obtain analogous results to the ones in this paper (show the existence of determinantal correlations, compute the correlation kernel and so on) for the parallel-update model but the situation is more involved
and we will not pursue the details here.

5.5.3
Continuous-time

For continuous time and countable state space an analogous framework for coupling intertwined semigroups was developed
20
20

20

In the earlier paper of Borodin-Ferrari
[
24
]
the continuous-time dynamics were obtained as a scaling limit from discrete-time.
by Borodin and Olshanski in
[
32
]
. The two-level dynamics are now defined through their explicit infinitesimal jump rates, see
[
32
]
, instead of their transition kernels which in continuous time are almost never explicit (unlike the explicit kernels (
80
), (
81
) for discrete-time). It can be checked that the dynamics coming from the coupling of
[
32
]
, associated to the intertwining from Theorem
4.11
, actually match the pure-birth push-block dynamics we have been considering in this paper. So in particular,
\mathsf{Q}^{N,N+1}_{\textnormal{exp}(-tw)}
is a rare example of an explicit transition kernel for the coupling framework developed in
[
32
]
.

6

Dynamics on arrays

6.1

The space-time inhomogeneous case

Combining our previous results we prove the following.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
N\geq 1
be arbitrary. Consider an initial configuration in
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
distributed according to

\mu_{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\mathfrak{L}_{N,N-1}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)}\right)\cdots\mathfrak{L}_{2,1}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\right),

(82)

for some probability measure
\mu_{N}
on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
. Then, perform
M_{1}
steps of sequential-update Bernoulli dynamics with parameters
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{M_{1}}
,
M_{2}
steps of Warren-Windridge geometric dynamics with parameters
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{M_{2}}
and finally continuous-time pure-birth dynamics for time
t
. The parameters
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
are assumed to satisfy
0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x})^{-1}
and
0\leq\beta_{i}<(\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x})^{-1}
. Then, the distribution of the resulting configuration in
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
is given by

\left[\mu_{N}\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{\prod_{i=1}^{M_{1}}(1-\alpha_{i}w)\prod_{i=1}^{M_{2}}(1+\beta_{i}w)^{-1}\exp(-tw)}\right]\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\mathfrak{L}_{N,N-1}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)}\right)\cdots\mathfrak{L}_{2,1}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\right).

(83)

Finally, consider the process
\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}^{(n)}(t);t\geq 0\right)_{1\leq i\leq n;1\leq n\leq N}
in
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
defined in Theorem
2.14
. Assume it is initialised according to (
82
). Then, for any
1\leq n\leq N
, the projection on the
n
-th row evolves as a Markov process with transition probabilities
\mathfrak{P}_{s,t}^{(n)}=\mathfrak{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(n)}
from (
12
).

Proof.

The proof is by induction, making use of Propositions
5.9
,
5.15
and
5.16
, by virtue of the Markov functions theory of Rogers-Pitman
[
126
]
. This type of argument, and variations, have been documented in many places
[
134
,
24
,
114
,
12
,
6
,
121
,
5
]
and we do not repeat all details. The main point is that under such dynamics, the Gibbs-type property of the initial distribution of the array, namely the fact that the law of the first
N-1
rows of the array given the
N
-th row being equal to
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
is given by:

\mathfrak{L}_{N,N-1}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)}\right)\mathfrak{L}_{N-1,N-2}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)},\mathbf{x}^{(N-2)}\right)\cdots\mathfrak{L}_{2,1}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\right),

is preserved for all times
t>0
, and the projections on single levels are Markovian with the desired transition probabilities
\mathfrak{P}_{s,t}^{(n)}=\mathfrak{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(n)}
. Then, the measure
\mu_{N}
on level
N
is evolved accordingly,

\displaystyle\left[\mu_{N}\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{(1-\alpha_{1}w)}\cdots\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{(1-\alpha_{M_{1}}w)}\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{(1+\beta_{1}w)^{-1}}\cdots\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{(1+\beta_{M_{2}}w)^{-1}}\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{\exp(-tw)}\right](\mathbf{x})=

\displaystyle\left[\mu_{N}\mathfrak{P}^{(N)}_{\prod_{i=1}^{M_{1}}(1-\alpha_{i}w)\prod_{i=1}^{M_{2}}(1+\beta_{i}w)^{-1}\exp(-tw)}\right](\mathbf{x}),

by virtue of Proposition
4.4
, which is where we need the (technical) assumption on the
\beta_{i}
(recall the assumption on the
\alpha_{i}
parameters is there for the transition probabilities to be well-defined). Observe that, the order in which we take the jumps is not important.
∎

We note that, the deterministic law of the fully-packed configuration is of the form (
82
) by taking
\mu_{N}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{1}_{(0,1,\dots,N-1)}(\mathbf{x})
.

6.2

The space-level inhomogeneous case

We now consider the space and level inhomogeneous setting of Theorem
2.24
. Our process evolves with either only continuous-time pure-birth dynamics, or only discrete time Bernoulli or only discrete time geometric dynamics which are all homogeneous in time. The extra new ingredient for the argument are the functions
\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}
, which are defined recursively in (
87
) and then shown to have a particular determinant expression in Proposition
6.9
which reveals the desired parameter symmetry. Other than that, most of the work has already been done.

Recall that, we write
\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}
with
\bullet\in\{\textnormal{pb, B, g}\}
, where the abbreviations stand for pure-birth, Bernoulli and geometric, to denote
\mathsf{T}_{f}
with the following choices of function
f(w)
: if
\bullet=\textnormal{pb}
, then
f(w)=\exp(-tw)
, if

\bullet=\textnormal{B}
, then
f(w)=(1-w)^{t}
and if
\bullet=\textnormal{g}
, then
f(w)=(1+w)^{-t}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

We have the following eigenfunction relations

\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}h_{\gamma}^{\bullet}(x)=c_{t,\gamma}^{\bullet}h_{\gamma}^{\bullet}(x),

where the functions
h_{\gamma}^{\bullet}
are given by

h_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{pb}}(x)=p_{x}(-\gamma),\ \ h_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{B}}(x)=p_{x}\left(1-\gamma^{-1}\right),\ \ h_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{g}}(x)=p_{x}(\gamma^{-1}-1),

and the constants
c_{t,\gamma}^{\bullet}
by,

c_{t,\gamma}^{\textnormal{pb}}=e^{t\gamma},\ \ c_{t,\gamma}^{\textnormal{B}}=\gamma^{-t},\ \ c_{t,\gamma}^{\textnormal{g}}=\gamma^{t}.

For
\bullet=\textnormal{g}
we require the technical condition
\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}-\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}<1
on
\mathbf{a}
. Moreover, if the parameter
\gamma
satisfies
\gamma\geq 0
for
\bullet=\textnormal{pb}
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
for
\bullet=\textnormal{B}
,
\gamma\geq 1
for
\bullet=\textnormal{g}
, then
h_{\gamma}^{\bullet}
is strictly positive.

Proof.

The eigenfunction relation in the pure-birth and Bernoulli cases follows directly from Lemma
3.7
. For the geometric case, subject to the condition on
\mathbf{a}
, it also follows from Lemma
3.7
but only for
\gamma
close to
1
. Instead we argue as follows. A direct computation using the explicit formula for
\mathsf{T}_{1}^{\textnormal{g}}
from Lemma
3.4
gives for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
,

\mathsf{T}_{1}^{\textnormal{g}}h_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{g}}(x)=c_{1,\gamma}^{\textnormal{g}}h_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{g}}(x).

Then, by virtue of Lemma
3.4
, subject to the condition on
\mathbf{a}
, we obtain the result for any
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. The final statement regarding positivity of
h^{\bullet}_{\gamma}(x)
is immediate from the fact that the polynomial
p_{x}
is strictly positive on
(-\infty,0]
. This gives a range of
\gamma
which is not optimal, but we restrict to it for simplicity.
∎

Recall Definition
2.27
.

Proposition 6.3
.

In the setting of Definition
2.27
we have,

\mathsf{T}_{t,\gamma}^{\bullet}(x,y)=\frac{1}{c_{t,\gamma}^{\bullet}}\frac{h_{\gamma}^{\bullet}(y)}{h_{\gamma}^{\bullet}(x)}\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}(x,y).

Proof.

A direct computation reveals this Doob transform relation between
\mathsf{T}_{t,\gamma}^{\bullet}
and
\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}
.
∎

Clearly, we have the relation

\mathsf{T}_{t,{\gamma_{1}}}^{\bullet}(x,y)=\frac{c_{t,{\gamma_{2}}}^{\bullet}}{c_{t,{\gamma_{1}}}^{\bullet}}\frac{h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(y)h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(x)}{h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(y)h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(x)}\mathsf{T}_{t,\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(x,y).

(84)

We then define the following kernels from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N}
by,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{N}v_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}},

(85)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\gamma_{N},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{N}v_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})\frac{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}{h_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}},

(86)

where
v_{\gamma}^{\bullet}(x)
is given by:

v_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{pb}}(x)=(a_{x}+\gamma)^{-1},\ v_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{B}}(x)=(\gamma a_{x}+1-\gamma)^{-1},\ v_{\gamma}^{\textnormal{g}}(x)=(\gamma a_{x}-1+\gamma)^{-1}.

Definition 6.4
.

Define the strictly positive function
\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}
on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
as follows:

\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=\left[\Lambda_{N,N-1}^{\gamma_{N},\gamma_{N-1},\bullet}\Lambda_{N-1,N-2}^{\gamma_{N-1},\gamma_{N-2},\bullet}\cdots\Lambda_{2,1}^{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1},\bullet}\mathbf{1}\right](\mathbf{x}).

(87)

For the rest of this section it will be convenient to use the following notation (note that
\gamma
here is a scalar)

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma,\bullet,N}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\det\left(\mathsf{T}^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

Observe that
\mathcal{P}^{\gamma,\bullet,N}_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
coincides with
\mathsf{P}_{f}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
from Definition
4.1
with the following choices of function
f
and inhomogeneity
\mathbf{a}
: if
\bullet=\textnormal{pb}
then
f(w)=\exp(-tw)
and the inhomogeneity is
\mathbf{a}+\gamma
, for
\bullet=\textnormal{B}
, then
f(w)=(1-w)^{t}
and the inhomogeneity is
\gamma\mathbf{a}+1-\gamma
and for
\bullet=\textnormal{g}
then
f(w)=(1-w)^{-t}
and the inhomogeneity is
\gamma\mathbf{a}+\gamma-1
. Here, for a scalar
c
,
\mathbf{a}+c
is simply the sequence
(a_{x}+c)_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
. Thus, by virtue of Theorem
4.6
we have the intertwining, with
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
,

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet,N+1}\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right)=\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet}\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet,N}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right).

From relation (
84
) we then obtain yet another intertwining:

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet,N+1}\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\gamma_{N},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right)=\left(\frac{c^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma_{N}}}{c^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma_{N+1}}}\right)^{N}\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\gamma_{N},\bullet}\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N},\bullet,N}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right).

(88)

Hence, by induction we obtain that

\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}
is a strictly positive eigenfunction of
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N},\bullet,N}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

We have:

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N},\bullet,N}\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}=\left(c^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma_{N}}\right)^{-(N-1)}\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}c^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma_{j}}\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}.

With all these preliminaries in place we arrive to the following definition.

Definition 6.6
.

For all
N\geq 1
, define the Markov kernel
\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}
from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N}
by

\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right)=\prod_{j=1}^{N}v_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})\frac{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}{h_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}\frac{\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}(\mathbf{x})}{\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N+1})}(\mathbf{y})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{y}},

(89)

and define abusing notation the Markov kernel
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}
from
\mathbb{W}_{N}
to itself by

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)=\left(c_{t,\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}\right)^{N-1}\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{c_{t,\gamma_{j}}^{\bullet}}\frac{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{x})}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{t,\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(90)

Here, we have abused notation since in principle we have already defined
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}
in (
15
). From expression (
90
) the symmetry in the parameters
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{N}
is not obvious at all due to the recursive definition of
\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}
from (
87
). Of course, we will prove shortly in Proposition
6.10
below that (
15
) and (
90
) are one and the same. The following intertwining is then immediate from (
88
), with
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N+1}\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right)=\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}\left(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\right),

(91)

and we also note the following explicit formulae.

Lemma 6.7
.

We have the expressions

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{c_{t,\gamma_{j}}^{\bullet}}\frac{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(y_{j})}{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}\frac{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{x})}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}

\displaystyle=\left(c^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma_{N+1}}\right)^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{c_{t,\gamma_{j}}^{\bullet}}\frac{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(y_{j})h_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})h_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(y_{j})}\frac{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{x})}\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet,N}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}).

We now obtain an alternative expression for
\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}
which will give us a final expression for
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}
from which parameter symmetry will be obvious. The following little observation is the key ingredient.

Lemma 6.8
.

We have the identity,
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
y>x
,

\sum_{m=x}^{y-1}\frac{v_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(m)h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(m)}{h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(m)}=c^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{2};\gamma_{1}}\left[\frac{h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(x)}{h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(x)}-\frac{h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(y)}{h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(y)}\right],

(92)

where the constant
c^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{2};\gamma_{1}}
is given by

c^{\textnormal{pb}}_{\gamma_{2};\gamma_{1}}=\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}},\ c^{\textnormal{B}}_{\gamma_{2};\gamma_{1}}=\frac{\gamma_{1}}{\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}},\ c^{\textnormal{g}}_{\gamma_{2};\gamma_{1}}=\frac{\gamma_{1}}{\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{1}}.

Proof.

This is proven by induction on
y
. For
y=x+1
an elementary computation gives

\frac{h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(x)}{h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(x)}-\frac{h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(x+1)}{h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(x+1)}=\frac{1}{c^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{2};\gamma_{1}}}\frac{v_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(x)h_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(x)}{h_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(x)}.

Then, we have a telescoping sum and the conclusion follows.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\right)=\prod_{j=2}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{j-1}c_{\gamma_{j};\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}\det\left(h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

Proof.

This is obtained by induction. For the inductive step we can bring the sums inside the determinant by multinearity and then use the identity (
92
) from Lemma
6.8
.
∎

By combining the above we obtain the final expression for
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}
matching (
15
).

Proposition 6.10
.

We have

\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{c_{t,\gamma_{j}}^{\bullet}}\times\frac{\det\left(h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\det\left(h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(93)

In particular,
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}
is symmetric in the parameters
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{N}
.

Proof.

Combine Lemma
6.7
and Proposition
6.9
.
∎

We need one more definition.

Definition 6.11
.

Define the sub-Markov kernel
\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}
from
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
to itself as follows:

1.

If
\bullet=\textnormal{pb}
, then with
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\textnormal{pb},\gamma_{N+1}}=\mathcal{Q}_{\exp(-tw)}^{N,N+1}
, with the underlying inhomogeneity sequence required to define
\mathcal{Q}_{\exp(-tw)}^{N,N+1}
being
\mathbf{a}+\gamma_{N+1}
.

2.

If
\bullet=\textnormal{B}
, then with
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\textnormal{B},\gamma_{N+1}}=\mathcal{Q}_{(1-w)^{t}}^{N,N+1}
, with the underlying inhomogeneity sequence required to define
\mathcal{Q}_{(1-w)^{t}}^{N,N+1}
being
\gamma_{N+1}\mathbf{a}-\gamma_{N+1}+1
.

3.

If
\bullet=\textnormal{g}
, we first define the sub-Markov kernel
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{N,N+1}
by

\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{N,N+1}\left[\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right),\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\right]=\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}\left[\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right),\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\right],

with the underlying inhomogeneity sequence defining
\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}
being
\gamma_{N+1}\mathbf{a}+\gamma_{N+1}-1
and
\beta=1
. Then, with
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\textnormal{g},\gamma_{N+1}}=\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{N,N+1}\cdots\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{N,N+1}
convolved with itself
t
times. .

Remark 6.12
.

The slightly more involved definition for the geometric case is because
\mathcal{G}^{N,N+1}
is already Markov as it is the analogue of
\mathsf{Q}_{f}^{N,N+1}
; in fact, as mentioned earlier, they should be equal for
f(z)=(1+\beta z)^{-1}
. So we need to unnormalise it first to obtain
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{N,N+1}
, the analogue of
\mathcal{Q}_{f}^{N,N+1}
, in order to treat all three cases uniformly.

The following is simply a re-writing of Propositions
5.9
,
5.15
and
5.16
in the uniform notation of
\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}
(but we note again that the way the parameter
\gamma_{N+1}
appears in the inhomogeneity sequence is different in each case).

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
t\geq 0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left[\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}\Pi_{N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Pi_{N}\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet,N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right),

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet,N+1}\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet}\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right),

where we view
\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet}
as a Markov kernel from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
as before.

Observe that, by virtue of Lemma
6.7
we can Doob transform the semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\gamma_{N+1},\bullet,N}
to
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}
using the eigenfunction

\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{h_{\gamma_{n}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}{h_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})

having eigenvalue
\left(c^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma_{N+1}}\right)^{-N}\prod_{j=1}^{N}c_{t,\gamma_{j}}^{\bullet}
. Hence, we can correctly define the Markov kernel
\mathsf{D}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}
on
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
by,

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\left(c^{\bullet}_{t,\gamma_{N+1}}\right)^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{c_{t,\gamma_{j}}^{\bullet}}\frac{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(y_{j})h_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})}{h_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})h_{\gamma_{N+1}}^{\bullet}(y_{j})}\times

\displaystyle\times\frac{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{x})}\mathfrak{W}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}\left[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right].

Putting everything together we obtain, by virtue of Proposition
6.13
:

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
N\geq 1
and
t\geq 0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{D}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}\Pi_{N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Pi_{N}\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}\right]\left((\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right),\ (\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(94)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N+1}\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\mathsf{D}_{t}^{N,N+1,\bullet,\gamma_{N+1}}\right]\left(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right),\ \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N+1},\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1},

(95)

where we view
\Lambda_{N+1,N}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}
as a Markov kernel from
\mathbb{W}_{N+1}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N,N+1}
as before.

Theorem
2.24
is then a direct consequence of the following proposition with the choice
\mu_{N}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{1}_{(0,1,\dots,N-1)}(\mathbf{x})
.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
N\geq 1
be arbitrary. Consider the process
\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}^{(n),\bullet}(t);t\geq 0\right)_{1\leq i\leq n;1\leq n\leq N}
from Definition
2.23
in
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
. We assume that the sequence
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
satisfies (
14
) and if
\bullet=\textnormal{B}
then
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}\leq 1
or if
\bullet=\textnormal{g}
then
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}<1
. Suppose the initial condition in
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
is of the form,

\mu_{N}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\Lambda_{N,N-1}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)}\right)\cdots\Lambda_{2,1}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\right),

(96)

for some probability measure
\mu_{N}
on
\mathbb{W}_{N}
. Then, for any
1\leq n\leq N
, the projection on the
n
-th row
\left(\mathsf{X}^{(n),\bullet}(t);t\geq 0\right)

evolves as a Markov process with transition probabilities
\left(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,n}\right)_{t\geq 0}
given in (
15
) and the distribution of
\left(\mathsf{X}^{(1),\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}),\dots,\mathsf{X}^{(N),\bullet}(\mathfrak{t})\right)
in
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
for fixed time
\mathfrak{t}
is given by

\left[\mu_{N}\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet,N}\right]\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\Lambda_{N,N-1}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(N)},\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)}\right)\cdots\Lambda_{2,1}^{\boldsymbol{\gamma},\bullet}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)}\right).

(97)

Proof.

Similarly to the proof of Proposition
6.1
this is obtained by induction using Proposition
6.14
now instead, see
[
134
,
24
]
for completely analogous arguments. As in Proposition
6.1
, the extra condition for
\bullet=\textnormal{B}
is so that the transition probabilities are positive and for
\bullet=\textnormal{g}
so that we can use our convolution of kernels result.
∎

7

Computation of the correlation kernels

By virtue of display (
83
) in Proposition
6.1
, with
\mu_{N}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}=(0,1,\dots,N-1)}
, a simple computation gives that, after running the dynamics described in Theorem
2.9
we can write out the resulting distribution of the array
\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}^{(n)}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n;1\leq n\leq N}
, for all
N\geq 1
, explicitly as a product of determinants. We then apply the Eynard-Mehta theorem
[
66
,
37
,
82
]
in the form that can be found in Lemma 3.4 of
[
26
]
(we do not recall this theorem explicitly as it has been documented many times). This immediately gives the existence of a determinantal point process structure. Finding an explicit expression for the correlation kernel
\mathfrak{K}_{f}
is our next task. We need to introduce some notation.

Definition 7.1
.

Define the functions
\Psi_{N-i}^{(N)}
, for
i=1,\dots,N
, by

\Psi_{N-i}^{(N)}(x)=-\frac{1}{a_{x}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{w^{N-i}f(w)}{p_{x+1}(w)}dw,\ x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

(98)

with
f(w)=\prod_{i=1}^{M_{1}}(1-\alpha_{i}w)\prod_{i=1}^{M_{2}}(1+\beta_{i}w)^{-1}\exp(-tw)
as first defined in (
11
).

Up to a multiplicative constant,
\mathfrak{P}_{f}^{(N)}((0,\dots,N-1),\mathbf{x})
is equal to
\det(\Psi_{N-j}^{(N)}(x_{i}))_{i,j=1}^{N}
. Note that, the choice of the
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
contour in the integral also gives the same functions
\Psi
(however the choice of
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}
will be important below). Define the kernel
\phi^{(n)}(x_{1},x_{2})
as the convolution of
\phi
from Lemma
4.3
with itself
n
times.

Definition 7.2
.

For
1\leq n\leq N-1
and
1\leq j\leq N
define the functions
\Psi_{n-j}^{(n)}
by convolution with
\phi^{(N-n)}
:

\Psi_{n-j}^{(n)}(y)=\left[\phi^{(N-n)}\Psi_{N-j}^{(N)}\right](y),\ y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

We will make use of the following intermediate lemmas.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
1\leq n\leq N
and
1\leq j\leq N
. Then, we have, with
f
as in (
11
),

\Psi_{n-j}^{(n)}(x)=-\frac{1}{a_{x}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{w^{n-j}f(w)}{p_{x+1}(w)}dw.

(99)

Proof.

We prove this by induction. We need to show

\left[\phi\Psi_{n-j}^{(n)}\right](x)=\Psi_{n-1-j}^{(n-1)}(x).

The left hand side is equal to

-\frac{1}{a_{x}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\sum_{y>x}-\frac{1}{a_{y}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{w^{n-1-j}f(w)}{p_{y+1}(w)}dw.

We deform the contour
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}
to the contour
\tilde{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathbf{a}}
from the proof of Lemma
3.5
. Then, we can bring the sum inside the integral, use identity (
40
) and deform the contour back to
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}
, without crossing any poles, which gives
\Psi_{n-1-j}^{(n-1)}(x)
and concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
1\leq k\leq N
. Then, we have

\phi^{(k)}(y,x)=-\frac{1}{a_{y}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{p_{y+1}(w)w^{k}}dw.

Proof.

This is Lemma 3.5 in
[
8
]
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
1\leq k\leq N
. Then, we have

\phi^{(k)}(\mathsf{virt},x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{w^{k}}dw.

Proof.

This is Lemma 3.6 in
[
8
]
.
∎

Definition 7.6
.

Let
1\leq n\leq N
and
1\leq j\leq n
. Define the following function, with
f
as in (
11
),

\Phi_{n-j}^{(n)}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{f(w)w^{n-j+1}}dw,\ x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(100)

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
1\leq n\leq N
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}\Psi_{i}^{(n)}(x)\Phi_{j}^{(n)}(x)=\mathbf{1}_{i=j},

for
0\leq i,j\leq n-1
.

Proof.

Observe that since
i
is non-negative we can use the
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
contour in the definition of
\Psi_{i}^{(n)}
. Then, by deforming, if required, the contour
\mathsf{C}_{0}
to a contour inside the region
\mathcal{U}
defined in (
35
), we can make use of Lemma
3.3
, to compute with
g(w)=f(w)w^{i}\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
where
R>R(\mathbf{a})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}\Psi_{i}^{(n)}(x)\Phi_{j}^{(n)}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}-\frac{1}{a_{x}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{w^{i}f(w)}{p_{x+1}(w)}dw\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}\frac{p_{x}(u)}{f(u)u^{j+1}}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}\frac{1}{f(u)u^{j+1}}\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}p_{x}(u)\mathsf{T}_{g}(x)du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C_{0}}}\frac{1}{f(u)u^{j+1}}f(u)u^{i}du=\mathbf{1}_{i=j},

as desired.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

The functions
\left\{\Phi_{j}^{(n)}(\cdot);0\leq j\leq n-1\right\}
span the space

\textnormal{span}\left\{\phi^{(1)}(\mathsf{virt},\cdot),\phi^{(2)}(\mathsf{virt},\cdot),\dots,\phi^{(n)}(\mathsf{virt},\cdot)\right\}.

Proof.

Using the Cauchy integral formula we see that

\textnormal{span}\left\{\phi^{(1)}(\mathsf{virt},\cdot),\phi^{(2)}(\mathsf{virt},\cdot),\dots,\phi^{(n)}(\mathsf{virt},\cdot)\right\}=\textnormal{span}\left\{x\mapsto\frac{d^{k-1}}{dw^{k-1}}p_{x}(w)\bigg|_{w=0};1\leq k\leq n\right\}.

Again, using the Cauchy integral formula, since
f
has no zeroes in
\mathsf{C}_{0}
, we have

\Phi_{j}^{(n)}(x)=\frac{1}{j!}\frac{d^{j}}{dw^{j}}\left(\frac{p_{x}(w)}{f(w)}\right)\bigg|_{w=0}=\frac{1}{j!}\frac{d^{j}}{dw^{j}}p_{x}(w)\bigg|_{w=0}+\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}c_{k,j}\frac{d^{k}}{dw^{k}}p_{x}(w)\bigg|_{w=0}.

Thus, we get

\textnormal{span}\left\{\Phi_{j}^{(n)}(\cdot);0\leq j\leq n-1\right\}=\textnormal{span}\left\{x\mapsto\frac{d^{k-1}}{dw^{k-1}}p_{x}(w)\bigg|_{w=0};1\leq k\leq n\right\}

as required.
∎

Lemma 7.9
.

For
1\leq n\leq N
, we have

\phi(\mathsf{virt},\cdot)=\Phi_{0}^{(n)}(\cdot)\equiv 1.

Proof.

This is due to the fact that
f(0)=1
and the fact that
f
has no zeroes in
\mathsf{C}_{0}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

We apply a variant of the Eynard-Mehta theorem, in exactly the form found in Lemma 3.4 of
[
26
]
, by virtue of all the preceding results in this section, to obtain the explicit form of the correlation kernel
\mathfrak{K}_{f}
as follows:

\mathfrak{K}_{f}\left[(n_{1},x_{1});(n_{2},x_{2})\right]=-\phi^{(n_{2}-n_{1})}(x_{1},x_{2})\mathbf{1}_{n_{2}>n_{1}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}\Psi_{n_{1}-k}^{(n_{1})}(x_{1})\Phi_{n_{2}-k}^{(n_{2})}(x_{2}).

We need to simplify the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}\Psi_{n_{1}-k}^{(n_{1})}(x_{1})\Phi_{n_{2}-k}^{(n_{2})}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{a_{x_{1}}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{f(w)w^{n_{1}-k}}{p_{x_{1}+1}(w)}dw\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}\frac{p_{x_{2}}(u)}{f(u)u^{n_{2}-k+1}}du

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{a_{x_{1}}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}dw\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}du\frac{p_{x_{2}}(u)f(w)}{p_{x_{1}+1}(w)f(u)}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{w^{n_{1}-k}}{u^{n_{2}-k+1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{a_{x_{1}}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}dw\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}du\frac{p_{x_{2}}(u)f(w)}{p_{x_{1}+1}(w)f(u)}\frac{w^{n_{1}}}{u^{n_{2}}}\frac{1}{w-u},

where we have extended the sum over
k
from
n_{2}
to infinity. This is allowed because there are no additional contributions for
k>n_{2}
since there are no poles in
u
in
\mathsf{C}_{0}
and thus the
u
-contour integral vanishes. Moreover, note that since
|u|<|w|
the geometric series converges. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

The functions
\mathfrak{h}_{N}
can be written as

\mathfrak{h}_{N}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)=\det\left(\phi^{(N+1-i)}\left(\mathsf{virt},x_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}=\det\left(\frac{1}{(i-1)!}\left(-\frac{d}{dw}\right)^{i-1}p_{x_{j}}(w)\big|_{w=0}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(101)

Proof.

This easily follows by the formula for
\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})
from Definition
4.7
.
∎

Proposition 7.11
.

Consider the process
\left(\mathsf{X}_{k}^{(n)}(t);t\geq 0\right)_{1\leq k\leq n;n\geq 1}
with discrete-time Bernoulli or geometric dynamics determined by the functions
f_{i,i+1}(z)
as in Theorem
2.14
. Let
M\geq 1
and
t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{M}
be arbitrary times. The joint distribution of
\left(\mathsf{X}^{(N)}(t_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{X}^{(N)}(t_{M})\right)
is then given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z}\det\left(\tilde{\Phi}^{(t_{1})}_{j}\left(x_{j}^{(1)}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\prod_{r=1}^{M-1}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{t_{r},t_{r+1}}}\left(x^{(r)}_{i},x^{(r+1)}_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left(\tilde{\Psi}^{(t_{M})}_{j}\left(x_{j}^{(M)}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N},

where the functions
\tilde{\Psi}_{j}
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{j}
are given by:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}^{(t_{1})}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{x}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{f_{0,t_{1}}(w)w^{j-1}}{p_{x+1}(w)}dw,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}^{(t_{M})}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{f_{0,t_{M}}(w)w^{j}}dw,

and
Z
is some normalisation constant. In the case of
\left(\mathsf{X}_{k}^{(n)}(t);t\geq 0\right)_{1\leq k\leq n;n\geq 1}
following the continuous-time pure-birth dynamics the conclusion is exactly the same but with
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
f_{t_{1},t_{2}}(z)=e^{-(t_{2}-t_{1})z}
instead.

Proof.

By virtue of Proposition
6.1
, we also note Lemma
7.10
,
\left(\mathsf{X}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
evolves as a Markov process with transition probabilities given by (
12
). Then, by using Markov property we can write down the following formula for the distribution of
\left(\mathsf{X}^{(N)}(t_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{X}^{(N)}(t_{M})\right)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{Z}}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{0,t_{1}}}\left(i-1,x^{(1)}_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{t_{1},t_{2}}}\left(x^{(1)}_{i},x^{(2)}_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\cdots\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{f_{t_{M-1},t_{M}}}\left(x^{(1)}_{i},x^{(2)}_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}\left(x^{(N)}\right),

for some normalisation constant
\tilde{Z}
. Then, by virtue of Lemma
7.8
(in particular, since the
\tilde{\Psi}_{i}^{(t_{M})}(z)
functions, which are essentially the
\Phi
functions from Definition
7.6
, span the space spanned by the
\phi^{(i)}(\mathsf{virt},z)
functions) and simple row operations on the determinants defining the first and last factors we obtain the desired result. Finally, the exact same argument gives the conclusion in the continuous-time case.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.14
.

It only remains to compute the correlation functions, as the probabilistic statement that projections on single levels are Markovian follows from Proposition
6.1
. Define, for
1\leq k\leq M
, the functions
\tilde{\Phi}_{i}^{(t_{k})}
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{j}^{(t_{k})}
by the convolutions

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{j}^{(t_{k})}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\tilde{\Phi}_{j}^{(t_{1})}\mathsf{T}_{f_{t_{1},t_{2}}}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{f_{t_{k-1},t_{k}}}\right]\left(x\right),

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{j}^{(t_{k})}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathsf{T}_{f_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{f_{t_{M-1},t_{M}}}\tilde{\Psi}_{j}^{(t_{M})}\right]\left(x\right).

Then, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
3.4
we obtain the following explicit description for them, for any
1\leq k\leq M
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}^{(t_{k})}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{x}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}\frac{f_{0,t_{k}}(w)w^{j-1}}{p_{x+1}(w)}dw,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}^{(t_{k})}_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}\frac{p_{x}(w)}{f_{0,t_{k}}(w)w^{j}}dw.

Finally, for any
1\leq k\leq M
, we have by an obvious adaptation of Proposition
7.7
,

\sum_{x=0}^{\infty}\tilde{\Phi}_{i}^{(t_{k})}(x)\tilde{\Psi}_{j}^{(t_{k})}(x)=\mathbf{1}_{i=j},

for
1\leq i,j\leq N
. Thus, by virtue of Proposition
7.11
and the Eynard-Mehta theorem in the form found for example in
[
19
]
, the correlation functions of the underlying point process are determinantal and the correlation kernel
\mathcal{K}_{N}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{N}\left[(s,x_{1});(t,x_{2})\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{1}_{t>s}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\tilde{\Psi}^{(s)}_{k}(x_{1})\tilde{\Phi}^{(t)}_{k}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{1}_{t>s}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)-\frac{1}{a_{x_{2}}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}dw\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}du\frac{p_{x_{1}}(u)f_{0,t}(w)}{p_{x_{2}+1}(w)f_{0,s}(u)}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{w^{k-1}}{u^{k}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{1}_{t>s}\mathsf{T}_{f_{s,t}}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)-\frac{1}{a_{x_{2}}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}dw\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}du\frac{p_{x_{1}}(u)f_{0,t}(w)}{p_{x_{2}+1}(w)f_{0,s}(u)}\frac{w^{N}}{u^{N}}\frac{1}{w-u},

where we have extended the sum over
k
to a sum from
-\infty
to
N
since for
k\leq 0
there are no poles in
u
in
\mathsf{C}_{0}
and thus no additional contributions to the sum. Moreover, since
|u|<|w|
the resulting geometric series is convergent. The proof for the continuous-time case is exactly the same but with
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
f_{s,t}(z)=e^{-(t-s)z}
instead.
∎

8

Walks conditioned to never intersect

In this section we prove Theorem
2.28
. The reader is advised to recall the corresponding notation and definitions therein. The following proposition is the main result of this section, from which Theorem
2.28
will easily follow.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
N\geq 1
be fixed and write
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})
. Consider the stochastic process
\left(\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t);t\geq 0\right)=\left(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(t),\dots,\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{N}}^{\bullet}(t);t\geq 0\right)
, with
\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(0)=\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
, with coordinates
\left(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(t);t\geq 0\right)
being independent and having transition probabilities
\left(\mathsf{T}_{t,\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}\right)_{t\geq 0}
. Assume that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})
, satisfy (
14
),
and for
i=1,\dots,N-1
, (
16
), (
17
), (
18
). Recall the first collision time
\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}
defined by

\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}=\inf\{t>0:\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t-)\nprec\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)\},

where if
\bullet\in\{\textnormal{B, g}\}
then
\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t-)=\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t-1)
while if
\bullet=\textnormal{pb}
then
\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t-)=\lim_{s\uparrow t}\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(s)
.
Then, we have the explicit expression, with
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}
denoting the law of
\left(\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t);t\geq 0\right)
starting from
\mathbf{x}
,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}=\infty\right)=\frac{\det\left(h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\prod_{i=1}^{N}h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{i})}.

(102)

Proof.

By a Doob-transform
[
61
,
125
]
of the corresponding LGV/Karlin-McGregor formula
[
90
,
69
]
we have

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\mathbf{y},\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}>t\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}c_{t,\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(y_{i})}{h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{i})}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}\left(x_{i},y_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

Thus, by summing over
\mathbb{W}_{N}
we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}>t\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}c_{t,\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(y_{i})}{h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{i})}\det\left(\mathsf{T}_{t}^{\bullet}\left(x_{i},y_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

By writing out the determinant explicitly in terms of the Leibniz formula and then instead of summing over
\mathbb{W}_{N}
we sum over
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
and subtract the sum over
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}\setminus\mathbb{W}_{N}
we get,

\displaystyle\frac{\det\left(h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\prod_{i=}^{N}h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{i})}-\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}(N)}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(y_{i})}{h_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(x_{i})}\mathbb{P}_{\left(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(N)}\right)}\left(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\bullet}(t)\notin\mathbb{W}_{N}\right).

Here,
\mathfrak{S}(N)
denotes the symmetric group on
N
symbols. We now proceed to show that
\mathbb{P}_{\left(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(N)}\right)}\left(\mathsf{X}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\bullet}(t)\notin\mathbb{W}_{N}\right)\to 0
, as
t\to\infty
, for any permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}(N)
. Observe that, it suffices to prove the following.
Suppose
\left(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}(t);t\geq 0\right)
and
\left(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}(t);t\geq 0\right)
are independent and follow the dynamics
\left(\mathsf{T}_{t,\gamma_{1}}^{\bullet}\right)_{t\geq 0}
and
\left(\mathsf{T}_{t,\gamma_{2}}^{\bullet}\right)_{t\geq 0}
respectively with initial conditions
\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{i}}^{\bullet}(0)=x_{i}
where
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are not necessarily ordered. Then, as
t\to\infty
we have,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{x}^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{1}}(t)<\mathsf{x}^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{2}}(t)\big|\left(\mathsf{x}^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{1}}(0),\mathsf{x}^{\bullet}_{\gamma_{2}}(0)\right)=(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\to 1,

(103)

which completes the proof. This last claim can be established as follows. Let us consider the pure-birth case. We can then couple
\left(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\textnormal{pb}}(t);t\geq 0\right),\left(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\textnormal{pb}}(t);t\geq 0\right)
with two independent standard Poisson processes
\left(\mathsf{y}_{1}(t);t\geq 0\right)
and
\left(\mathsf{y}_{2}(t);t\geq 0\right)
of rates
\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+\gamma_{1}
and
\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+\gamma_{2}
respectively such that almost surely,

\mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\textnormal{pb}}(t)\geq\mathsf{y}_{2}(t),\ \ \mathsf{x}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\textnormal{pb}}(t)\leq\mathsf{y}_{1}(t),\forall t\geq 0.

Note that, since
\mathsf{y}_{1},\mathsf{y}_{2}
are standard Poisson processes we have almost surely

\frac{\mathsf{y}_{1}(t)}{t}\overset{t\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+\gamma_{1},\ \ \frac{\mathsf{y}_{2}(t)}{t}\overset{t\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+\gamma_{2}.

Thus, we readily obtain

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{x}^{\textnormal{pb}}_{\gamma_{1}}(t)<\mathsf{x}^{\textnormal{pb}}_{\gamma_{2}}(t)\big|\left(\mathsf{x}^{\textnormal{pb}}_{\gamma_{1}}(0),\mathsf{x}^{\textnormal{pb}}_{\gamma_{2}}(0)\right)=(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{y}_{1}(t)<\mathsf{y}_{2}(t)|(\mathsf{y}_{1}(0),\mathsf{y}_{2}(0))=(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\overset{t\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}1,

if we have
\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+\gamma_{2}>\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}+\gamma_{1}
which proves the claim. Finally, the Bernoulli and geometric cases follow by the exact same argument by comparing to the corresponding homogeneous dynamics.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

We note that, as long as one has (
103
) then the whole argument goes through. The coupling with the homogeneous dynamics, which is where the conditions (
16
), (
17
), (
18
) are required, gives a much stronger asymptotic statement than the one needed to establish (
103
).

From our previous results (but a direct proof is also possible) we easily obtain strict positivity for the probability we just computed.

Proposition 8.3
.

We have, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
, with
\bullet\in\{\textnormal{pb},\textnormal{B},\textnormal{g}\}
and the
\gamma_{i}
parameters satisfying the corresponding conditions in Proposition
8.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}=\infty\right)>0.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the formula given in Proposition
8.1
above along with the recursive formula for
\mathsf{H}_{(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N})}^{\bullet}
found in (
87
) and Proposition
6.9
.
∎

Putting everything together we obtain Theorem
2.28
.

Proof of Theorem
2.28
.

Observe that, by conditioning on
\tau^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{col}}>t+s
, using the strong Markov property and taking
s\to\infty
(by virtue of Proposition
8.3
there are no issues of dividing by
0
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{X}^{\bullet,\textnormal{n.c.}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\mathbf{y}\big|\mathsf{X}^{\bullet,\textnormal{n.c.}}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(0)=\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\mathbf{y}\big|\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}=\infty\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{y}}\left(\tau^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{col}}>s\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\tau^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{col}}>t+s\right)}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\mathbf{y},\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}>t\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{y}}\left(\tau^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{col}}=\infty\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\tau^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{col}}=\infty\right)}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(\mathsf{X}^{\bullet}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(t)=\mathbf{y},\tau_{\textnormal{col}}^{\bullet}>t\right).

The conclusion then follows by plugging in the explicit formula from Proposition
8.1
.
∎

9

Transition kernels and determinantal processes for the edge particle systems for general initial condition

In this section we prove Theorem
2.32
, by building on our preceding results, as Theorem
9.5
and Theorem
9.6
below.

Define the Markov kernel
\mathfrak{L}^{(N)}
from
\mathbb{W}_{N}
to
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
by the formula

\mathfrak{L}^{(N)}\left(\mathbf{x},\left(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{y}^{(N)}\right)\right)=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{y}^{(N)}=\mathbf{x}}\prod_{n=1}^{N-1}\mathfrak{L}_{n+1,n}\left(\mathbf{y}^{(n+1)},\mathbf{y}^{(n)}\right).

(104)

Also, define
\hat{\mathfrak{L}}^{(N)}\left(\mathbf{x},\left(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{y}^{(N)}\right)\right)=\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\mathfrak{L}^{(N)}\left(\mathbf{x},\left(\mathbf{y}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{y}^{(N)}\right)\right)
. Recall also the definition of
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}
from (
19
) which is the state space for
\left(\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(N)}(t),\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(N-1)}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(1)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
. We denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\textnormal{r}}^{(N)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\textnormal{l}}^{(N)}
the projections on the right and left edges of
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
respectively:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\textnormal{r}}^{(N)}\left[\left(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(x_{1}^{(1)},x_{2}^{(2)},\dots,x_{N}^{(N)}\right)\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\textnormal{l}}^{(N)}\left[\left(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(N)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(x_{1}^{(N)},x_{1}^{(N-1)},\dots,x_{1}^{(1)}\right)\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}.

We can view
\mathcal{E}_{\textnormal{r}}^{(N)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\textnormal{l}}^{(N)}
as Markov kernels from
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N}
and
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}
respectively. Finally, define the Markov kernels
\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{r}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{l}}
from
\mathbb{W}_{N}
to
\mathbb{W}_{N}
and
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}
respectively by the compositions:

\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{r}}=\mathfrak{L}^{(N)}\mathcal{E}^{(N)}_{\textnormal{r}},\ \ \mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{l}}=\mathfrak{L}^{(N)}\mathcal{E}^{(N)}_{\textnormal{l}}.

The proposition below is our starting point.

Proposition 9.1
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.14
, with the notations and assumptions therein, recall that we denote by
\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{r}}^{(N)}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{l}}^{(N)}
the transition kernels from time
s
to time
t
of the autonomous systems
\left(\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(1)}(t),\mathsf{X}_{2}^{(2)}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{N}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
and
\left(\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(N)}(t),\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(N-1)}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(1)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
on the right and left edge of the array. Then, we have the intertwinings

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{r}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{r}}\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{r}}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\ \ \mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

(105)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{l}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{l}}\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{l}}^{(N)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\mathbf{y}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}.

(106)

Proof.

Let us write
\mathfrak{IA}^{(N)}_{f_{s,t}}
for the transition probabilities from time
s
to time
t
of the Markov chain
\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}^{(n)}(t);t\geq 0\right)_{1\leq i\leq n;1\leq n\leq N}
in
\mathbb{IA}_{N}
from Theorem
2.14
. Then, since the evolution of
\left(\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(1)}(t),\mathsf{X}_{2}^{(2)}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{N}^{(N)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
and
\left(\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(N)}(t),\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(N-1)}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{1}^{(1)}(t);t\geq 0\right)
is autonomous we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{IA}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}\mathcal{E}^{(N)}_{\textnormal{r}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{(N)}_{\textnormal{r}}\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{r}}^{(N)},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{IA}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}\mathcal{E}^{(N)}_{\textnormal{l}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{(N)}_{\textnormal{l}}\mathfrak{E}_{f_{s,t},\textnormal{l}}^{(N)}.

On the other hand, by virtue of Proposition
6.1
we have

\mathfrak{P}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}\mathfrak{L}^{(N)}=\mathfrak{L}^{(N)}\mathfrak{IA}_{f_{s,t}}^{(N)}.

Combining the equations in the above displays we obtain the desired statement.
∎

We define, for
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\psi_{\textnormal{r}}(x,y)=a_{y}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{x\leq y}\ \textnormal{ and }\ \psi_{\textnormal{l}}(x,y)=a_{y}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{y<x}.

Write
\psi_{\textnormal{r}}^{n}(x,y)
for the convolution of
\psi_{\textnormal{r}}(x,y)
with itself
n
times, with
\psi^{0}_{\textnormal{r}}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{x=y}
, and similarly for
\psi_{\textnormal{l}}^{n}(x,y)
. Define the kernels
\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{r}}
,
\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{l}}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{r}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{r}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\ \ \mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{l}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\mathsf{E}_{N,\textnormal{l}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\mathbf{y}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}.

Proposition 9.2
.

\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{r}}
has the following determinant expression, with
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,

\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{r}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\det\left(\psi_{\textnormal{r}}^{N-j}(x_{i},y_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{N},

(107)

and similarly
\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{l}}
, with
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}
is given by

\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{l}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\det\left(\psi_{\textnormal{l}}^{j-1}(x_{i},y_{j}))\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(108)

Proof.

Observe that, we have

\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{r}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)}\\
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}=\mathbf{x};\ (x_{1}^{(1)},x_{2}^{(2)},\dots,x_{N}^{(N)})=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\prec\mathbf{x}^{(i+1)}}\prod_{j=1}^{i}\frac{1}{a_{x_{j}^{(i)}}},\ \ \mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}.

We now observe that, each term in the sum above is in correspondence with a set of
N
non-intersecting paths in a certain LGV graph
[
69
]
. The LGV graph is given by the square grid
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{N}
with horizontal edges directed to the right and vertical edges directed down. All horizontal edges have weight
1
and all vertical edges with horizontal co-ordinate
x
have weight
a_{x}^{-1}
. We are then looking at
N
non-intersecting paths from
(x_{1},N),\dots,(x_{N},N)
to
(y_{1},1),\dots,(y_{N},N)
which end with a vertical edge, see Figure
12
for an illustration. Hence, by the LGV formula
[
69
]
we obtain

\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{r}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\det\left(\textnormal{Weight of such path }(x_{i},N)\to(y_{j},j)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N},\ \mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}.

Finally, it is not hard to see that in this LGV graph

\textnormal{Weight of such path }(x_{i},N)\to(y_{j},j)=\psi_{\textnormal{r}}^{N-j}(x_{i},y_{j})

and this completes the proof for
\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{r}}
. We now turn our attention to the left edge. Observe that,

\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{l}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(N-1)}\\
\mathbf{x}^{(N)}=\mathbf{x};\ (x_{1}^{(N)},x_{1}^{(N-1)},\dots,x_{1}^{(1)})=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{x}^{(i)}\prec\mathbf{x}^{(i+1)}}\prod_{j=1}^{i}\frac{1}{a_{x_{j}^{(i)}}},\ \ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\mathbf{y}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}.

We then observe that, each term in the sum above is in correspondence with a set of
N
non-intersecting paths in a different LGV graph
[
69
]
. This graph has vertex set
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{N}
. It has horizontal edges from
(x+1,n)
to
(x,n)
, namely directed to the left, of weight
1
. Moreover, it has diagonal edges directed from
(x+1,n+1)
to
(x,n)
of weight
a_{x}^{-1}
. We are then looking at
N
non-intersecting paths from
(x_{1},N),\dots,(x_{N},N)
to
(y_{1},N),\dots,(y_{N},1)
ending with a diagonal edge, see Figure
12
for an illustration. Then, by the LGV
[
69
]
formula we get

\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{l}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\det\left(\textnormal{Weight of such path }(x_{i},N)\to(y_{j},N-j+1)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N},\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\mathbf{y}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{N}.

Finally, we see that in this LGV graph

\textnormal{Weight of such path }(x_{i},N)\to(y_{j},N-j+1)=\psi_{\textnormal{l}}^{j-1}(x_{i},y_{j})

and this gives the desired expression for
\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{N,\textnormal{l}}
and completes the proof.
∎
