\mathfrak{p}(N+M)-2
\mathfrak{p}(N+M)-1

Figure 27:
An illustration of the stochastic process

\left(\mathsf{X}_{0}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t),\mathsf{X}_{1}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{\mathfrak{p}N-1}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t);t=1,\dots,L-1\right),

which is basically the random point configuration, depicted as filled circles, corresponding to the probability measure (
129
). We can then join the points with the same subscript by straight lines as explained in the text to obtain a non-intersecting line ensemble. We can extend this to include fixed starting and final positions
(0,1,\dots,\mathfrak{p}N-2,\mathfrak{p}N-1)
and
(\mathfrak{p}M,\mathfrak{p}M+1,\dots,\mathfrak{p}(N+M)-2,\mathfrak{p}(N+M)-1)
at times
0
and
L
which are depicted as blue filled circles. We note that this line ensemble should not be confused with the non-intersecting paths in LGV graphs from previous sections. This structure, if it exists, will depend on the specifics of the functions
\mathbf{f}_{i}
, compare for example with Figure
7
. Theorem
13.4
states that the finite dimensional distributions of this stochastic process can be written explicitly in terms of determinants of a correlation kernel which involves the solution of an explicit Riemann-Hilbert problem. Theorem
13.7
below states that, under certain assumptions, as
N\to\infty
, the bottom
m
lines, for any
m\geq 1
, converge.

Definition 13.3
.

Define the function
\mathbf{f}_{r,r^{\prime}}
for
r<r^{\prime}
by
\mathbf{f}_{r,r^{\prime}}(z)=\left[\mathbf{f}_{r}\mathbf{f}_{r+1}\cdots\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime}-1}\right](z)
and the complex matrix-valued measure
\mathbf{M}(z)=\mathbf{M}(z;\mathbf{a})
, which also depends on
L
and
M+N
but we suppress this in the notation, by

\mathbf{M}(z;\mathbf{a})=\frac{\left[\mathbf{f}_{0}\mathbf{f}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{f}_{L-1}\right](z)}{p_{M+N}(z;\mathbf{a})}.

Let
\mathbf{A}^{\textnormal{t}}
denote the transpose of a matrix
\mathbf{A}
and let
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the zero and identity
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrices respectively. We have the following theorem which generalises the results of Section 4 of
[
62
]
to
a_{x}\nequiv 1
.

Theorem 13.4
.

Let
M\geq 0
,
\mathfrak{p}\geq 1
,
L\geq 2
. Suppose that the matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{f}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{f}_{L-1}
have entries in
\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
for
R>R(\mathbf{a})
. Assume that the expression (
129
) defines a probability measure on
(L-1)
copies of
\mathbb{W}_{\mathfrak{p}N}
. Denote by the corresponding stochastic processes of non-intersecting paths. Then, this is described by a determinantal point process, namely for any
n\geq 1
and pairwise distinct time-space points
(t_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(t_{n},x_{n})
in
\llbracket 1,L-1\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\ j_{1},\dots,j_{n}\textnormal{ such that }\mathsf{X}_{j_{i}}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t_{i})=x_{i}\textnormal{ for }i=1,\dots,n\right)=\det\left(\mathsf{K}_{N}\left[(t_{i},x_{i});(t_{j},x_{j})\right]\right)_{i,j=1}^{n},

(131)

where the correlation kernel
\mathsf{K}_{N}
given by

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{K}_{N}\left[(r,m\mathfrak{p}+j);(r^{\prime},k\mathfrak{p}+i)\right]\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}=\mathbf{1}_{r>r^{\prime}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{m}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},r}(z)\frac{p_{k}(z)}{p_{m+1}(z)}dz

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\frac{1}{a_{k}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},L}(w)\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)\mathbf{f}_{0,r}(z)\frac{p_{k}(w)}{p_{M+N}(w)p_{m+1}(z)}dzdw.

(132)

Here, the
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
is given by

\mathbf{R}_{N}(z,w)=\frac{1}{z-w}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ \mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{Y}^{-1}(w)\mathbf{Y}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}

(133)

with the
2\mathfrak{p}\times 2\mathfrak{p}
matrix-valued function
\mathbf{Y}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\mathfrak{p}\times 2\mathfrak{p}}
being the unique solution to the following Riemann-Hilbert problem (RHP) of size
2\mathfrak{p}\times 2\mathfrak{p}
:

•

\mathbf{Y}
is analytic,

•

\mathbf{Y}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{Y}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{M}(z;\mathbf{a})\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}
on
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
, where
\mathbf{Y}_{+}
is the limit of
\mathbf{Y}(z)
from inside
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{-}
the limit of
\mathbf{Y}(z)
from outside
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
respectively,

•

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})\right)\begin{pmatrix}z^{N}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&z^{-N}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}
as
z\to\infty
.

Remark 13.5
.

We observe that for
a_{x}\equiv 1
, and after the change of variables
z\mapsto 1-z
,
w\mapsto 1-w
Theorem
13.4
specialises to Theorem 4.7 of
[
62
]
with the identifications, in the notations of
[
62
]
,
\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},r}(1-z)=A_{r^{\prime},r}(z)
,
1-\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}=\gamma
and
\mathbf{R}_{N}(1-w,1-z)=-R_{N}(w,z)
.

Remark 13.6
.

Positivity of (
129
) is in some sense not strictly necessary as long as the normalisation constant
Z\neq 0
. One then has a signed measure of total mass
1
on point configurations (but there is no associated stochastic process of course). We can still define correlation functions for such signed measures, see
[
19
]
. The analysis that follows essentially goes through and the conclusions of Theorem
13.4
on explicit determinantal correlations remain valid.

Using the above theorem and some Riemann-Hilbert problem asymptotic analysis, under a factorisation assumption for
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(z)
and some assumptions on the sequence
\mathbf{a}
, we obtain the following limit theorem for the bottom paths as
N\to\infty
. This generalises Theorem 3.1 of
[
16
]
to
a_{x}\neq 1
. Let us define
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
, with
\eta\in(0,1)
, to be the annulus
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\eta<|z|<\eta^{-1}\right\}
about
0
.

Theorem 13.7
.

Assume that the parameter sequence
\mathbf{a}
satisfies

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}\geq 1-\mathfrak{c}\textnormal{ and }\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}\leq 1+\mathfrak{c},

(134)

for some
0\leq\mathfrak{c}<\frac{1}{3}
. Suppose that the matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{f}_{0}(z),\dots,\mathbf{f}_{L-1}(z)
have entries in
\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
for
R>R(\mathbf{a})
and moreover that the functions
\mathbf{f}_{0}(1-z)^{\pm 1},\dots,\mathbf{f}_{L-1}(1-z)^{\pm 1}
are analytic in an annulus
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
where
\eta<1-2\mathfrak{c}
. Finally, assume we have the factorisations
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)=\mathbf{S}_{+}(z)\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)=\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(z)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(z)
, where
\mathbf{S}_{\pm}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{\pm}
are
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrix-valued functions satisfying

•

\mathbf{S}_{+}^{\pm 1}(z),\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}^{\pm 1}(z)
are analytic for
|z|<1
and continuous for
|z|\leq 1
,

•

\mathbf{S}_{-}^{\pm 1}(z),\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}^{\pm 1}(z)
are analytic for
|z|>1
and continuous for
|z|\geq 1
,

•

\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)\sim z^{M}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(z)\sim z^{M}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as
z\to\infty
.

Consider the line ensemble
\left(\mathsf{X}_{0}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t),\mathsf{X}_{1}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{\mathfrak{p}N-1}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t);t=1,\dots,L-1\right)

associated to (
129
). Then, for any
m\geq 1
, we have the following convergence in distribution for the bottom
m
lines in the line ensemble as
N\to\infty
, with
m\leq\mathfrak{p}N
,

\left(\mathsf{X}_{0}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{m-1}^{N,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t);1\leq t\leq L-1\right)\overset{\textnormal{d}}{\longrightarrow}\left(\mathsf{X}_{0}^{\infty,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t),\dots,\mathsf{X}_{m-1}^{\infty,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t);1\leq t\leq L-1\right),

where the limiting line ensemble
\left(\left(\mathsf{X}_{i}^{\infty,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t)\right)_{i=0}^{\infty};t=1,\dots,L-1\right)
is again determined through its determinantal correlation functions: for any
n\geq 1
and pairwise distinct time-space points
(t_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(t_{n},x_{n})
in
\llbracket 1,L-1\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\ j_{1},\dots,j_{n}\textnormal{ such that }\mathsf{X}_{j_{i}}^{\infty,\mathfrak{p},L,M}(t_{i})=x_{i}\textnormal{ for }i=1,\dots,n\right)=\det\left(\mathsf{K}_{\infty}\left[(t_{i},x_{i});(t_{j},x_{j})\right]\right)_{i,j=1}^{n},

(135)

where the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{\infty}
is given by:

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{K}_{\infty}\left[(r,m\mathfrak{p}+j);(r^{\prime},k\mathfrak{p}+i)\right]\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}=-\mathbf{1}_{r>r^{\prime}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{m}}\oint_{|z|=1}\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},r}(1-z)\frac{p_{k}(1-z)}{p_{m+1}(1-z)}dz

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\frac{1}{a_{k}}\oint_{|z|=1^{-}}\oint_{|w|=1^{+}}\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},L}(1-w)\mathbf{S}_{-}^{-1}(w)\mathbf{S}_{+}^{-1}(z)\mathbf{f}_{0,r}(1-z)\frac{p_{k}(1-w)}{p_{m+1}(1-z)}\frac{dzdw}{z-w}.

Remark 13.8
.

Although the functions
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}
(for
\mathfrak{p}=1
these can simply be picked the same as
\mathbf{S}_{-}
,
\mathbf{S}_{+}
) do not appear in the limiting kernel
\mathsf{K}_{\infty}
, they are required for the proof. The proof unsurprisingly boils down to computing the asymptotics of
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
as
N\to\infty
. Note that, along with the polynomial
p_{M+N}(\cdot)
this is the only
N
-dependent quantity in
\mathsf{K}_{N}
.

Remark 13.9
.

In
[
16
]
the authors also consider a limit theorem for the top paths in the line ensemble (in this case
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}
appear in the limiting kernel instead). In our case, if we consider the same scaling, we would need to enforce the inhomogeneity parameters
\mathbf{a}
to be asymptotically constant to get a limit (which essentially puts us back into the setting of
[
16
]
). It is plausible that interesting limit theorems exist for the top paths for general
\mathbf{a}
but under a different scaling. We leave this for future work.

We first prove Theorem
13.4
. The following lemma is essential for computations.

Lemma 13.10
.

Let
\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}
be
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrix-valued functions such that all their entries belong to
\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
, for
R>R(\mathbf{a})
. Then, we have
\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{g}}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}\mathbf{g}}
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward extension of the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

Remark 13.11
.

One may hope that Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
have analogous straightforward extensions for matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{f}
but as far as we can tell this is not the case.

By virtue of Lemma
13.10
we obtain that convolutions of the
\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r}}
operators satisfy

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r+1}}\cdots\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime}-1}}\left(k\mathfrak{p}+i,m\mathfrak{p}+j\right)\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}=\left[\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r,r^{\prime}}}\left(k\mathfrak{p}+i,m\mathfrak{p}+j\right)\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}.

Definition 13.12
.

Let
\mathbf{G}=(\mathbf{G}_{\kappa\mu})_{\kappa,\mu=0}^{\mathfrak{p}N-1}
be the so-called Gram matrix associated to this problem given by, by virtue of Lemma
13.10
,

\mathbf{G}_{\kappa\mu}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{0}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{1}}\dots\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{L-1}}(\kappa,\mathfrak{p}M+\mu)=\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{0,L}}\left(\kappa,\mathfrak{p}M+\mu\right).

(136)

Then, note that
\det(\mathbf{G})=Z>0
, by our assumption that (
129
) is a well-defined probability measure, and thus
\mathbf{G}
is invertible. Moreover, observe that we can write

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\kappa\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{M+m}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}p_{k}(z)\mathbf{M}(z)_{ij}\frac{p_{M+N}(z)}{p_{M+m+1}(z)}dz

(137)

\displaystyle\textnormal{ if }\kappa=k\mathfrak{p}+i,\mu=m\mathfrak{p}+j,\ \textnormal{ for }i,j=0,\dots,\mathfrak{p}-1\textnormal{ and }k,m=0,\dots,N-1.

Now, given invertible
\mathfrak{p}N\times\mathfrak{p}N
matrices
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q}
we define (slightly abusing notation)
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrix-valued orthogonal polynomials
\mathbf{P}_{j},\mathbf{Q}_{j}
, for
j=0,\dots,N-1
, of degree at most
N-1
(namely their entries have degrees at most
N-1
) as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{P}_{0}(z)\\
\mathbf{P}_{1}(z)\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{P}_{N-1}(z)\\
\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{P}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
p_{1}(z)\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\vdots\\
p_{N-1}(z)\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\end{pmatrix},\ \ \begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{0}(z)\\
\mathbf{Q}_{1}(z)\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{Q}_{N-1}(z)\\
\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{Q}\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{a_{M}}\frac{p_{M+N}(z)}{p_{M+1}(z)}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
-\frac{1}{a_{M+1}}\frac{p_{M+N}(z)}{p_{M+2}(z)}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\vdots\\
-\frac{1}{a_{M+N-1}}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

The following result relates inverting the matrix
\mathbf{G}
to finding biorthogonal matrix-valued polynomials.

Proposition 13.13
.

Suppose
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q}
are invertible
\mathfrak{p}N\times\mathfrak{p}N
matrices and define
\mathbf{P}_{j},\mathbf{Q}_{j}
matrix-valued polynomials as above. Let
\mathbf{G}
be the Gram matrix defined in (
136
). Then, the following are equivalent

1.

\mathbf{G}^{-1}=\mathbf{Q}^{\textnormal{t}}\mathbf{P}

2.

For
j,k=0,\dots,N-1
,

\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{P}_{j}(z)\mathbf{M}(z)\mathbf{Q}_{k}^{\textnormal{t}}(z)dz=\mathbf{1}_{j=k}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}.

Proof.

We adapt the proof of Proposition 4.5 in
[
62
]
. The key is to consider the matrix

\mathbf{X}=\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{P}_{0}(z)\\
\mathbf{P}_{1}(z)\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{P}_{N-1}(z)\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}^{\textnormal{t}}_{0}(z)\ \mathbf{Q}^{\textnormal{t}}_{1}(z)\cdots\mathbf{Q}^{\textnormal{t}}_{N-1}(z)\end{pmatrix}dz

and observe that
\mathbf{P}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Q}^{-\textnormal{t}}=\mathbf{G}
from which the conclusion follows.
∎

For any factorization
\mathbf{G}^{-1}=\mathbf{Q}^{\textnormal{t}}\mathbf{P}
we define the reproducing kernel
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
, which we will show in the sequel, in the proof of Theorem
13.4
, that it can be written as (
133
), by

\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{Q}_{j}^{\textnormal{t}}(w)\mathbf{P}_{j}(z).

Observe that,
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
is independent of the choice of factorization
\mathbf{Q}^{\textnormal{t}}\mathbf{P}
since

\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{a_{M}}\frac{p_{M+N}(w)}{p_{M+1}(w)}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ -\frac{1}{a_{M+1}}\frac{p_{M+N}(w)}{p_{M+2}(w)}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdots-\frac{1}{a_{M+N-1}}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
p_{1}(z)\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\vdots\\
p_{N-1}(z)\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(138)

The following proposition gives the reproducing property of
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
which also characterises it.

Proposition 13.14
.

For any matrix-valued polynomial
\mathbf{S}(z)
of degree at most
N-1
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{S}(w)\mathbf{M}(w)\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)dw

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}(z),

(139)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)\mathbf{M}(z)\mathbf{S}^{\textnormal{t}}(z)dz

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}^{\textnormal{t}}(w).

(140)

Moreover, any bivariate polynomial of degree at most
N-1
satisfying either equality above for all matrix-valued polynomials
\mathbf{S}(z)
of degree at most
N-1
must be equal to
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
.

Proof.

The proof is a word for word adaptation of the proof of Lemma 4.6 in
[
62
]
.
∎

We finally need the following.

Proposition 13.15
.

There is a unique monic matrix-valued polynomial
\mathbf{P}_{N}
:

\mathbf{P}_{N}(z)=z^{N}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}+\cdots

of degree
N
such that

\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{P}_{N}(z)\mathbf{M}(z)z^{k}dz=\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}},\ \ k=0,\dots,N-1.

Proof.

We expand (note that we can write
z^{j}
as a linear combination of
p_{0}(z),\dots,p_{j}(z)
, see also the proof of Proposition
3.10
)

\mathbf{P}_{N}(z)=\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}z^{N}+\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{C}_{j}p_{j}(z)

for some unknown
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrices
\mathbf{C}_{j}
that we would like to solve for. Showing that this is possible proves the proposition. Plugging in the above expansion in the orthogonality relation we obtain the following equations for
\mathbf{C}_{j}
,

\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\mathbf{C}_{j}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}p_{j}(z)\mathbf{M}(z)z^{k}dz=-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}z^{N}\mathbf{M}(z)z^{k}dz,\ \ k=0,\dots,N-1.

Let us write
\mathbf{U}
for the
\mathfrak{p}N\times\mathfrak{p}N
matrix having
j
-th,
k
-th block, for
j,k=0,\dots,N-1
, given by the
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrix

\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}p_{j}(z)\mathbf{M}(z)z^{k}dz.

If we show that
\mathbf{U}
is invertible then we are done since by inverting it we can solve for
\mathbf{C}_{j}
. Recall that
\mathbf{G}
is a
\mathfrak{p}N\times\mathfrak{p}N
matrix having
j
-th,
k
-th block, for
j,k=0,\dots,N-1
, given by the
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrix

\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}p_{j}(z)\mathbf{M}(z)\left(-\frac{1}{a_{M+k+1}}\right)\frac{p_{M+N}(z)}{p_{M+k+1}(z)}dz

and it is invertible, by our assumption that (
129
) is a well-defined probability measure. Let us write
\mathbf{V}
for the
\mathfrak{p}N\times\mathfrak{p}N
change of basis matrix given by

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ z\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdots z^{N-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{a_{M}}\frac{p_{M+N}(z)}{p_{M+1}(z)}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ -\frac{1}{a_{M+1}}\frac{p_{M+N}(z)}{p_{M+2}(z)}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdots-\frac{1}{a_{M+N-1}}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{V}.

Clearly
\mathbf{V}
is invertible. Thus, since we can write
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{G}\mathbf{V}
the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
13.4
.

By the Eynard-Mehta theorem, for example in the form presented in
[
19
]
, we have that the induced point process from (
129
) is determinantal and the correlation kernel is given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{N}\left[(r,x);(r^{\prime},y)\right]=-\mathbf{1}_{r>r^{\prime}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},r}}(y,x)+\sum_{\kappa,\mu=0}^{\mathfrak{p}N-1}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{0,r}}(\kappa,x)\mathbf{G}^{-\textnormal{t}}_{\kappa\mu}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},L}}(y,\mathfrak{p}M+\mu).

(141)

Then, the first term (more precisely the
i,j
-th coordinate) is easily seen to be given by the first term in the expression in the statement of the theorem. So let us focus on the second term involving the sum over
\kappa,\mu
which we denote by
\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{N}
. Let us write
\kappa=\mathfrak{p}\nu_{1}+\delta_{1}
and
\mu=\mathfrak{p}\nu_{2}+\delta_{2}
. Then, this term becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{N}[(r,m\mathfrak{p}+j);(r^{\prime},k\mathfrak{p}+i)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}=0}^{N-1}\sum_{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},L}}(k\mathfrak{p}+i,\mathfrak{p}M+\mathfrak{p}\nu_{2}+\delta_{2})\mathbf{G}^{-1}_{\mathfrak{p}\nu_{2}+\delta_{2},\mathfrak{p}\nu_{1}+\delta_{1}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathbf{f}_{0,r}}(\mathfrak{p}\nu_{1}+\delta_{1},m\mathfrak{p}+j).

We can write this in block matrix form as follows

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\mathsf{K}}_{N}[(r,m\mathfrak{p}+j);(r^{\prime},k\mathfrak{p}+i)]\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}

\displaystyle=\left[-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{M+\nu_{2}}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},L}(w)\frac{p_{k}(w)}{p_{M+\nu_{2}+1}(w)}dw\right]_{\nu_{2}=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{G}^{-1}\left[-\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{m}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{f}_{0,r}(z)\frac{p_{\nu_{1}}(z)}{p_{m+1}(z)}dz\right]_{\nu_{1}=0}^{N-1},

where the first factor above is a block row vector having length
N
with
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
blocks and the second factor a block column vector of the corresponding size. Combining the integrals and recalling the expression for
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
from (
138
) gives the expression of the correlation kernel in the statement of the theorem.

It remains to show that
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
can also be written as (
133
). Towards this end, following
[
62
,
57
]
, we note that the size
2\mathfrak{p}\times 2\mathfrak{p}
Riemann-Hilbert problem in the statement of the theorem for
\mathbf{Y}(z)
has a unique solution given by:

\mathbf{Y}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{P}_{N}(z)&\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{\mathbf{P}_{N}(s)\mathbf{M}(s)}{s-z}ds\\
\mathbf{Q}_{N-1}(z)&\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\frac{\mathbf{Q}_{N-1}(s)\mathbf{M}(s)}{s-z}ds\end{pmatrix},\ \ z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}},

where
\mathbf{P}_{N}(z)
is the monic matrix-valued polynomial from Proposition
13.15
and
\mathbf{Q}_{N-1}
is the matrix-valued polynomial of degree at most
N-1
satisfying

\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathbf{Q}_{N-1}(z)\mathbf{M}(z)z^{k}dz=\begin{cases}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}},&k=0,\dots,N-2,\\
-\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}},&k=N-1.\end{cases}

The fact that
\mathbf{Q}_{N-1}
exists and is unique is proven by following the same scheme of proof as for Proposition
13.15
, by solving for the corresponding coefficient matrices.
Finally, the fact that
\mathbf{R}_{N}(w,z)
has a representation as in (
133
) is proven in exactly the same way as in Proposition 4.9 of
[
62
]
: by checking that this expression satisfies the characterising reproducing property from Proposition
13.14
. This completes the proof.
∎

Moving on to the proof of Theorem
13.7
the following proposition is the key technical ingredient. Analogous results should hold for a more general class of parameters
\mathbf{a}
.

Proposition 13.16
.

Assume that the parameter sequence
\mathbf{a}
satisfies

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}\geq 1-\mathfrak{c}\textnormal{ and }\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}\leq 1+\mathfrak{c},

(142)

for some
0\leq\mathfrak{c}<\frac{1}{3}
. For
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
define the constant
\mathfrak{c}_{r}
,

\mathfrak{c}_{r}=\frac{\mathfrak{c}+r}{1-\mathfrak{c}}<1.

(143)

Moreover, assume that the matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{f}_{0}(1-z)^{\pm 1},\dots,\mathbf{f}_{L-1}(1-z)^{\pm 1}
are analytic in an annulus
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
where
\eta<1-2\mathfrak{c}
. Finally, assume we have the factorisations
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)=\mathbf{S}_{+}(z)\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)=\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(z)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(z)
, where
\mathbf{S}_{\pm}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{\pm}
are
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrix-valued functions satisfying

•

\mathbf{S}_{+}^{\pm 1}(z),\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}^{\pm 1}(z)
are analytic for
|z|<1
and continuous for
|z|\leq 1
,

•

\mathbf{S}_{-}^{\pm 1}(z),\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}^{\pm 1}(z)
are analytic for
|z|>1
and continuous for
|z|\geq 1
,

•

\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)\sim z^{M}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(z)\sim z^{M}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as
z\to\infty
.

Consider the
2\mathfrak{p}\times 2\mathfrak{p}
matrix-valued function
\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\{|z|=1\}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\mathfrak{p}\times 2\mathfrak{p}}
given as the unique solution to the
2\mathfrak{p}\times 2\mathfrak{p}
Riemann-Hilbert problem:

•

\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}
is analytic,

•

\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{+}(z)=\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}
on
|z|=1
,

•

\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})\right)\begin{pmatrix}z^{N}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&z^{-N}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}
as
z\to\infty
.

Then, as
N\to\infty
and for
|z|<1-2\mathfrak{c}
, we have

\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{N}\right)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(z)\\
-\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{S}_{+}^{-1}(z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

for any
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
such that
\max\{|z|,\eta\}<r
. Similarly, as
N\to\infty
and for
|z|>(1-2\mathfrak{c})^{-1}
,

\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{N}\right)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}^{-1}(z)p_{M+N}(1-z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

for any
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
such that
r^{-1}<\min\{|z|,\eta^{-1}\}
.

Proof.

We transform, in several steps, the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)
to a problem which can be solved explicitly, up to asymptotically negligible terms, by virtue of the factorisation for
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)
. The strategy itself is standard and was followed in
[
16
]
but there are a couple of interesting complications due to the non-constant inhomogeneity sequence
\mathbf{a}
.

Step 1
We make the following transformation to normalise (in a slightly less standard way) the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)
at infinity. Namely, define
\mathbf{X}(z)
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(z)=\begin{cases}\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)\begin{pmatrix}p_{M}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&p_{M+N}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},&|z|>1,\\
\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z),&|z|<1.\end{cases}

Then, a simple computation shows that
\mathbf{X}(z)
solves the Riemann-Hilbert problem with conditions, since
p_{m}(1-z)\sim\left(\prod_{k=0}^{m-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)z^{m}
as
z\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}p_{M+N}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&p_{M}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle|z|=1,

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\prod_{k=M}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\prod_{k=M}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\textnormal{ as }z\to\infty.

Step 2
We make another transformation. Consider
\mathbf{V}(z)
given by, where
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
is picked such that the circles
|z|=r
and
|z|=r^{-1}
are within the annulus of analyticity
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
from the statement, see Figure
28
for an illustration,

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{X}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
p_{M}^{2}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}&\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},&1<|z|<r^{-1},\\
\mathbf{X}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
-p_{M+N}(1-z)\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}&\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}&r<|z|<1,\\
\mathbf{X}(z),&|z|<r\textnormal{ or }|z|>r^{-1}.\end{cases}

Then, a computation shows that
\mathbf{V}(z)
solves the following RHP

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
-\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}p_{M+N}(1-z)&\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle|z|=r,

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\\
-\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}p_{M}(1-z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle|z|=1,

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
p_{M}^{2}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}&\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle|z|=r^{-1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\prod_{k=M}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\prod_{k=M}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\textnormal{ as }z\to\infty.

0
\eta
r
1
r^{-1}
\eta^{-1}

Figure 28:
The contours for the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\mathbf{V}
. The area enclosed in the dotted circles is the annulus
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
.

Step 3
Now, consider the following RHP problem for
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}(z)
, which disregards the jumps on
|z|=r
and
|z|=r^{-1}
for
\mathbf{V}(z)
, jumps that we will show next are exponentially small as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\\
-\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}p_{M}(1-z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle|z|=1,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{V}}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\prod_{k=M}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\prod_{k=M}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\textnormal{ as }z\to\infty.

Then, by our factorisation assumption on
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)
, we can construct the explicit solution to this RHP for
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}(z)
by, since recall
p_{M}(1-z)\sim\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)z^{M}
as
z\to\infty
,

\tilde{\mathbf{V}}(z)=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}^{-1}(z)p_{M}(1-z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\ &|z|>1,\\
\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(z)\\
-\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{S}_{+}^{-1}(z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix},\ &|z|<1.\end{cases}

(144)

Step 4
We now show that the jumps on the circles
|z|=r
and
|z|=r^{-1}
for
\mathbf{V}(z)
are exponentially small. Let
||\cdot||
be any norm on
\mathfrak{p}\times\mathfrak{p}
matrices. Then, for
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
picked such that the circles
|z|=r
and
|z|=r^{-1}
are within the annulus of analyticity
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
of
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}
, we have, with the implicit constant being independent of
N
and
r
,

\displaystyle\sup_{|z|=r}\left|\left|\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}p_{M+N}(1-z)\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}\right)^{N}}\left(\sup_{|z|=r}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}|z+a_{k}-1|\right)^{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(1-\mathfrak{c})^{N}}\left(\sup_{|z|=r}\sup_{x\in[-\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}]}|z+x|\right)^{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\mathfrak{c}+r}{1-\mathfrak{c}}\right)^{N}\right),

and similarly,

\displaystyle\sup_{|z|=r^{-1}}\left|\left|\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)^{-1}p_{M}^{2}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\right|\right|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{k}\right)^{N}\left(\sup_{|z|=r^{-1}}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\frac{1}{|z+a_{k}-1|}\right)^{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left((1+\mathfrak{c})^{N}\left(\frac{1}{\inf_{|z|=r^{-1}}\inf_{x\in[-\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}]}|z+x|}\right)^{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{1+\mathfrak{c}}{r^{-1}-\mathfrak{c}}\right)^{N}\right).

Observe that, for
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
with
0\leq\mathfrak{c}<\frac{1}{3}
, we have

\mathfrak{c}_{r}=\frac{\mathfrak{c}+r}{1-\mathfrak{c}}=\max\left\{\frac{\mathfrak{c}+r}{1-\mathfrak{c}},\frac{1+\mathfrak{c}}{r^{-1}-\mathfrak{c}}\right\}<1.

Now, consider the function
\mathbf{J}(z)
given by

\mathbf{J}(z)=\mathbf{V}(z)\tilde{\mathbf{V}}(z)^{-1}.

Then, observe that
\mathbf{J}(z)
, which only has jumps on the circles
|z|=r
and
|z|=r^{-1}
which are exponentially small, can be solved in terms of a Neumann series, see for example
[
56
]
, to give

\mathbf{J}(z)=\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{N}\right),\textnormal{ as }N\to\infty,

uniformly on compact sets of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\left(\{|z|=r\}\cup\{|z|=r^{-1}\}\right)
. This completes this step.

Step 5
We finally undo the transformations to obtain the statement of the proposition. For
|z|<1-2\mathfrak{c}
we can choose
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
such that
\max\{|z|,\eta\}<r
, to obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)=\mathbf{X}(z)=\mathbf{V}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{N}\right)\right)\tilde{\mathbf{V}}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{N}\right)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(z)\\
-\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{S}_{+}^{-1}(z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}.

For
|z|>(1-2\mathfrak{c})^{-1}
we can choose
r\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
such that
r^{-1}<\min\{|z|,\eta^{-1}\}
, to obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}(z)\begin{pmatrix}p_{M}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&p_{M+N}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}(z)\begin{pmatrix}p_{M}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&p_{M+N}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{N}\right)\right)\tilde{\mathbf{V}}(z)\begin{pmatrix}p_{M}(1-z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&p_{M+N}(1-z)p_{M}(1-z)^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{r}^{N}\right)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}^{-1}(z)p_{M+N}(1-z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)p_{M+N}(1-z)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

This completes the proof.
∎

We can finally prove Theorem
13.7
.

Proof of Theorem
13.7
.

Note that, by our assumption on
\mathbf{a}
we can pick the
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a}}
contour to be the circle
|z-1|=1
. After the change of variables
z\mapsto 1-z
,
w\mapsto 1-w
, we can then write the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{N}
presented in block matrix form in (
132
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{K}_{N}\left[(r,m\mathfrak{p}+j);(r^{\prime},k\mathfrak{p}+i)\right]\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}=-\mathbf{1}_{r>r^{\prime}}\frac{1}{2\pi\textnormal{i}}\frac{1}{a_{m}}\oint_{|z|=1}\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},r}(1-z)\frac{p_{k}(1-z)}{p_{m+1}(1-z)}dz

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\frac{1}{a_{k}}\oint_{|z|=1}\oint_{|w|=1}\mathbf{f}_{r^{\prime},L}(1-w)\frac{1}{z-w}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ \mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{-1}(w)\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{f}_{0,r}(1-z)\times

\displaystyle\times\frac{p_{k}(1-w)}{p_{M+N}(1-w)p_{m+1}(1-z)}dzdw,

where
\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}
is the Riemann-Hilbert problem given in Proposition
13.16
. We then deform the contour for
w
to a circle of radius at least
(1-2\mathfrak{c})^{-1}
but still within the annulus of analyticity
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
and similarly deform the contour for
z
to a circle of radius at most
(1-2\mathfrak{c})
but also contained in
\mathfrak{A}_{\eta}
. Note that, by our assumption this is possible. Hence, from Proposition
13.16
we have for any
\delta\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
such that
\delta^{-1}<\min\{|w|,\eta^{-1}\}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p_{M+N}(1-w)}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ \mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{-1}(w)=\frac{1}{p_{M+N}(1-w)}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ \mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{\delta}^{N}\right)\right)\times

\displaystyle\times\begin{pmatrix}\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(w)p_{M+N}(1-w)^{-1}&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\mathbf{S}_{-}^{-1}(w)p_{M+N}(1-w)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\ \mathbf{S}_{-}^{-1}(w)\end{pmatrix}\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{\delta}^{N}\right)\right),

and similarly for any
\delta\in(\mathfrak{c},1-2\mathfrak{c})
such that
\max\{|z|,\eta\}<\delta
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}(z)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{\delta}^{N}\right)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}&\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}\right)\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(z)\\
-\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{S}_{+}^{-1}(z)&\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\left(\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}_{2\mathfrak{p}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{c}_{\delta}^{N}\right)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\mathfrak{p}}\\
\mathbf{S}_{+}^{-1}(z)\end{pmatrix}.

Plugging these expressions in the correlation kernel
\mathsf{K}_{N}
, noting that
\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}^{-1}
and
\prod_{k=0}^{M+N-1}a_{k}
cancel out exactly, and taking the
N\to\infty
limit first (recall
\mathfrak{c}_{\delta}<1
), and then deforming the contours to the circles
|z|=1^{-}
and
|w|=1^{+}
we get

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left[\mathsf{K}_{N}\left[(r,m\mathfrak{p}+j);(r^{\prime},k\mathfrak{p}+i)\right]\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}=\left[\mathsf{K}_{\infty}\left[(r,m\mathfrak{p}+j);(r^{\prime},k\mathfrak{p}+i)\right]\right]_{i,j=0}^{\mathfrak{p}-1}.

This implies the convergence of all the correlation functions and since the state space is discrete it implies convergence in distribution of the corresponding point processes, and thus of the minimal particles, and gives the statement of the theorem.
∎

We finally give an explicit example where all the conditions of Theorem
13.7
are satisfied. We focus on the scalar case
\mathfrak{p}=1
with functions corresponding to the three types of non-intersecting processes (pure-birth, Bernoulli, geometric) we have been studying in this paper. Recall that for
\mathfrak{p}=1
we can just take
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{\pm}(z)=\mathbf{S}_{\pm}(z)
. For a detailed study of the factorisation in the matrix case
\mathfrak{p}\geq 2
, see
[
16
]
, also
[
22
,
45
]
.

Proposition 13.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}=1
. Assume that the parameter sequence
\mathbf{a}
satisfies

\inf_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}\geq 1-\mathfrak{c}\textnormal{ and }\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}\leq 1+\mathfrak{c},

(145)

for some
0\leq\mathfrak{c}<\frac{1}{3}
. Suppose the functions
\mathbf{f}_{0}(z),\dots,\mathbf{f}_{L-1}(z)
are a product of factors of the form
(1-\alpha z),\ (1+\beta z)^{-1},\ e^{-tz}
so that in particular the function
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)
is given by

\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)=\prod_{r=1}^{L_{1}}(1-\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{r}z)\prod_{r=1}^{L_{2}}\frac{1}{1+\beta_{r}-\beta_{r}z}\prod_{r=1}^{L_{3}}e^{t_{r}z-t_{r}},

for some
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and constants
\alpha_{r},\beta_{r},t_{r}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Moreover, suppose there exist exactly
M
indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{M}
such that

(2-2\mathfrak{c})^{-1}<\alpha_{i_{j}}<(1+\mathfrak{c})^{-1},\ \ j=1,\dots,M,

and for
r\neq i_{j}
we have
\alpha_{r}<\frac{1-2\mathfrak{c}}{2-2\mathfrak{c}}
. Finally, assume that
\beta_{r}<\frac{1}{2\mathfrak{c}}-1
. Then, with the factorisation
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(1-z)=\mathbf{S}_{+}(z)\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)
chosen as follows

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left(z+\frac{1-\alpha_{i_{j}}}{\alpha_{i_{j}}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{i_{j}}\prod_{r=1,r\neq i_{j}}^{L_{1}}(1-\alpha_{r}+\alpha_{r}z)\prod_{r=1}^{L_{2}}\frac{1}{1+\beta_{r}-\beta_{r}z}\prod_{r=1}^{L_{3}}e^{t_{r}z-t_{r}},

all the conditions of Theorem
13.7
are satisfied.

Proof.

Observe that,
\mathbf{f}_{i}(1-z)^{\pm 1}
can have poles only at points
(1-\alpha_{r}^{-1})
or
(1+\beta_{r}^{-1})
for some
r
. Then, elementary computations show that the conditions above on the
\alpha_{r},\beta_{r}
ensure that
\mathbf{f}_{0,L}(z)\in\mathsf{Hol}\left(\mathbb{H}_{-R}\right)
for some
R>R(\mathbf{a})=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}a_{x}
and that we can find
\eta<1-2\mathfrak{c}
such that none of the points
(1-\alpha_{r}^{-1})
or
(1+\beta_{r}^{-1})
are in the intervals
(-\eta^{-1},-\eta)\cup(\eta,\eta^{-1})
.
Finally, it is easy to see by the assumptions on the
\alpha_{r},\beta_{r}
’s the functions
\mathbf{S}^{\pm 1}_{-}(z)
,
\mathbf{S}^{\pm 1}_{+}(z)
are analytic for
|z|>1
and
|z|<1
respectively (and continuous up to the boundary) and we have the correct growth condition as
z\to\infty
for
\mathbf{S}_{-}(z)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.50
.

Note that, the induced measure on
\mathbb{W}_{N}^{L-1}
by the non-intersecting random walks is given by (
129
) with
\mathfrak{p}=1
and
\mathbf{f}_{r}=f_{r,r+1}
as in the statement of Theorem
2.50
. Then, the result follows by combining Theorem
13.7
and Proposition
13.17
with
\mathsf{K}_{\infty}^{L,M}=\mathsf{K}_{\infty}
.
∎

14

Convergence to the discrete Bessel point process

Proof of Theorem
2.20
.

Let us denote by
\tilde{K}_{t}^{(N)}(y_{1},y_{2})
the correlation kernel of the point process with the origin shifted to
N
, namely
\left\{\mathsf{X}_{i}^{(N)}(t)-N\right\}_{i}
, which is then given by from Theorem
2.14
, where we make a couple of changes of variables in the contour integrals,

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{t}^{(N)}(y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{a_{y_{1}+N}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}dw\oint_{\mathsf{C}_{0}}du\frac{p_{y_{2}+N}(u)e^{-tw}}{p_{y_{1}+N+1}(w)e^{-tu}}\frac{w^{N}}{u^{N}}\frac{1}{w-u}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{y_{1}+N}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\oint_{1-\mathsf{C}_{\mathbf{a},0}}dw\oint_{1-\mathsf{C}_{0}}du\frac{p_{y_{2}+N}(1-u)e^{tw}}{p_{y_{1}+N+1}(1-w)e^{tu}}\frac{(1-w)^{N}}{(1-u)^{N}}\frac{1}{w-u}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{y_{1}+N}}\frac{1}{(2\pi\textnormal{i})^{2}}\oint_{|w|=\epsilon^{-1}}dw\oint_{|u|=(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}}du\frac{p_{y_{2}+N}(1-u)e^{tw}}{p_{y_{1}+N+1}(1-w)e^{tu}}\frac{(1-w)^{N}}{(1-u)^{N}}\frac{1}{w-u}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{{y_{1}+N}}}\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\textnormal{i}\right)^{2}}\oint_{|w|=\epsilon}dw\oint_{|u|=\epsilon^{\prime}}du\frac{p_{y_{2}+N}\left(1-\frac{1}{u}\right)}{p_{y_{1}+N+1}\left(1-\frac{1}{w}\right)}\frac{e^{tw^{-1}}}{e^{tu^{-1}}}\frac{u^{N-1}}{w^{N+1}}\frac{\left(1-w\right)^{N}}{\left(1-u\right)^{N}}\frac{1}{u-w},

with the radii
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}
chosen appropriately small so that the circles
|w|=\epsilon^{-1}
and
|z|=(\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}
contain all the relevant poles. Let us pick, for concreteness, for
N
large enough,
\epsilon=\frac{\zeta}{N}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{2\zeta}{N}
and make the change of variables
v=\frac{N}{\zeta}u
and
z=\frac{N}{\zeta}w
. We then obtain that the kernel is given by, for
N
large enough,

\displaystyle\tilde{K}^{(N)}_{t}\left(y_{1},y_{2}\right)=\frac{1}{a_{{y_{1}+N}}}\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\textnormal{i}\right)^{2}}\oint_{|z|=1}dz\oint_{|v|=2}dv\frac{p_{y_{2}+N}\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)}{p_{y_{1}+N+1}\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)}\frac{e^{\frac{tN}{\zeta}z^{-1}}}{e^{\frac{tN}{\zeta}v^{-1}}}\frac{v^{N-1}}{z^{N+1}}\frac{\left(1-\frac{\zeta z}{N}\right)^{N}}{\left(1-\frac{\zeta v}{N}\right)^{N}}\frac{1}{v-z}\frac{N}{\zeta}.

Consider the ratio of characteristic polynomials

\frac{p_{y_{2}+N}\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)}{p_{y_{1}+N+1}\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)}=\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}{\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}\times\frac{\prod_{k=N}^{y_{2}+N-1}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}{\prod_{k=N}^{y_{1}+N}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}.

(146)

The second factor above satisfies, where as usual
\mathfrak{f}_{N}\sim\mathfrak{g}_{N}
means
\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathfrak{f}_{N}}{\mathfrak{g}_{N}}=1
,

\frac{\prod_{k=N}^{y_{2}+N-1}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}{\prod_{k=N}^{y_{1}+N}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}\sim\frac{\left(\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)^{y_{2}}}{\left(\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)^{y_{1}+1}}\frac{\prod_{k=N}^{y_{1}+N}a_{k}}{\prod_{k=N}^{y_{2}+N-1}a_{k}}=\frac{\left(\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)^{y_{2}}}{\left(\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)^{y_{1}+1}}\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{y_{1}+N}a_{k}}{\prod_{k=0}^{y_{2}+N-1}a_{k}}.

Recall that
\{a_{x}\}_{x=0}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded. If we write
a_{i}^{(N)}=N^{-1}\left(\zeta a_{i}-N\right)
, for
1\leq i\leq N
, then we have
a_{i}^{(N)}\to 0
for all
i
,
\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}^{(N)}\to\zeta(\bar{a}-1)
and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}(a_{i}^{(N)})^{2}\to 0
. We then note that, uniformly on compact sets in
\mathbb{C}
, we have as
N\to\infty
,

\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1+v\left(\frac{\zeta a_{k}-\zeta}{N}\right)\right)=\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1+va_{i}^{(N)}\right)\to e^{\zeta(\bar{a}-1)v}.

(147)

Hence, we obtain that the first factor in (
146
) satisfies, as
N\to\infty
,

\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}{\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)}{a_{k}}\right)}=\frac{z^{N}}{v^{N}}\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1+v\left(\frac{\zeta a_{k}-\zeta}{N}\right)\right)}{\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1+z\left(\frac{\zeta a_{k}-\zeta}{N}\right)\right)}\sim\frac{z^{N}}{v^{N}}\frac{e^{\zeta(\bar{a}-1)v}}{e^{\zeta(\bar{a}-1)z}}.

Thus, we get the asymptotics as
N\to\infty
,

\frac{v^{N-1}}{z^{N+1}}\frac{p_{y_{2}+N}\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta v}\right)}{p_{y_{1}+N+1}\left(1-\frac{N}{\zeta z}\right)}\frac{N}{\zeta}\sim\frac{z^{y_{1}}}{v^{y_{2}+1}}\frac{e^{\zeta(\bar{a}-1)v}}{e^{\zeta(\bar{a-1})z}}\left(\frac{N}{\zeta}\right)^{y_{2}-y_{1}}\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{y_{1}+N}a_{k}}{\prod_{k=0}^{y_{2}+N-1}a_{k}}.

Hence, putting everything together we obtain, as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{\frac{\zeta}{N}}^{(N)}\left(y_{1},y_{2}\right)\sim\left(\frac{N}{\zeta}\right)^{y_{2}-y_{1}}\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{y_{1}+N-1}a_{k}}{\prod_{k=0}^{y_{2}+N-1}a_{k}}\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\textnormal{i}\right)^{2}}\oint_{|z|=1}dz\oint_{|v|=2}dv\frac{z^{y_{1}}}{v^{y_{2}+1}}e^{z^{-1}-v^{-1}+\zeta\bar{a}v-\zeta\bar{a}z}\frac{1}{v-z}.

Observe that the ratio of factors in front of the contour integral cancel out when we take the determinant. Moreover, we can deform the
z
and
v
contours as long as the
z
-contour is contained in the
v
-contour and contains
0
. Hence, for any
m\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\det\left(\tilde{K}_{\frac{\zeta}{N}}^{(N)}\left(y_{i},y_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{m}\overset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\det\left(\mathbf{J}_{\zeta\bar{a}}\left(y_{i},y_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{m}.

This implies the convergence of all the correlation functions and since the state space is discrete it implies convergence in distribution of the corresponding point processes, and thus of the maximal particles, and gives the statement of the theorem.

∎
